Parametrised functor calculus: excision, spheres, and semiadditivity
Kaif Hilman, Sil Linskens

Abstract
We lay down the foundations of a theory of parametrised functor calculus , generalising parts of the functor calculus of Goodwillie. We introduce the notion of excisable posets and develop a theory of excisive approximations in this context. As an application, we introduce two different excisable posets when parametrising over an atomic orbital category. By comparing the notions of excisiveness for these two posets, we relate the invertibility of certain spheres with Nardin’s notion of parametrised semiadditivity, generalising Wirthmüller’s classical result in equivariant homotopy theory for finite groups.

1
Introduction

Multiplicative structures give rise to some of the deepest problems and methods in homotopy theory. As such, analysis of functors such as
X\mapsto X^{\otimes n}
and its variants are of great interest in this field. Fortunately, there is a far–reaching and systematic strategy to study such functors in the form of Goodwillie’s
functor calculus

[
9
,
10
,
11
]
, which is by now part of the standard repertoire of methods in higher algebra. Analogously, subtle multiplicative structures in the form of the norm functors of
[
12
,
13
]
often play a central role in nontrivial results coming from equivariant higher algebra as well as in their parametrised generalisations
[
1
,
2
,
29
,
26
,
28
,
24
]
. One may thus ask for a suitable generalisation of calculus adapted to this setting in service of understanding the native polynomial phenomena therein. In this direction, Blumberg
[
3
]
and Dotto–Moi
[
7
]
have studied the notion of equivariant linearity, and this was then extended to a fully fledged theory of equivariant Goodwillie calculus by Dotto
[
8
]
in the setting of model categories.

In this work, we make a first jab at a general theory of
parametrised functor calculus
by developing a flexible framework of excision, inspired in part by Stoll’s formulation in his study of functor calculus for non–cubes
[
30
]
. The basic building blocks of our theory will be the notion of a
parametrised excisable poset
, which we introduce in more detail below. As we explain there, we believe this definition captures the crucial structure of cubes used in classical Goodwillie calculus.
Working in this generality leads to a presentation of the basic theory of calculus which only uses simple manipulations of left and right Kan extension along adjunctions which exist already at the level of posets
1
1

1

Our arguments often boil down to aligning various stars (and shrieks), used to denote Kan extensions. In this sense, our proofs are often pure “astrology”, a phrase which we learnt from Shachar Carmeli.
and do not have to resort to more subtle cofinality arguments. The reason for this is in part aesthetic, but is also motivated by the more serious issue that many inductive proofs about cubes in ordinary Goodwillie calculus are not available to us when dealing with indexed products in the parametrised setting, and so we are forced to produce “coordinate–free” proofs for much of the elementary cubical facts. As such, the techniques employed in this article may be of independent interest.

As a proof of concept, we employ our rudimentary theory to obtain a parametrised generalisation of the classical fact in equivariant homotopy theory that inverting the representation spheres yields the so-called Wirthmüller isomorphism. This will be achieved by constructing appropriate “parametrised spheres” using the cubes introduced in
[
16
]
. We consider this result to be slightly surprising, as it removes all of the geometry of the Pontryagin–Thom collapse which is crucial to traditional approaches to the Wirthmüller isomorphism.

Excisable posets and excision

Cubes constitute the grammar for Goodwillie’s theory in that an “
n
–excisive functor” (this should be compared to polynomials of degree at most
n
in ordinary algebra) is defined to be one which sends certain kinds of cocartesian
(n+1)
–cubes to cartesian ones. That this admits a rich theory mirroring many intuitions from ordinary algebra and at the same time captures many deep phenomena in homotopy theory is nothing short of miraculous. Even more amazingly, many of his methods are so robust that they are amenable to vast levels of generalisation.

One such generalisation is the recent work of Stoll
[
30
]
who extended Goodwillie’s definition of excisiveness to special posets he termed as
shapes
which are not necessarily cubes. It was shown in
[
30
, Thm. A]
that the excisive approximations defined against shapes admit a formula akin to Goodwillie’s original formula for
n
–excisive approximations. As will be clear to the reader familiar with Stoll’s work, our setup and language in
Section

2
is heavily inspired by his. However, our methods are rather different: instead of using shapes as the building blocks for our theory, we opt for an alternative approach using what we term as
excisable posets
. As mentioned, the advantage of this approach is that many proofs may be done very formally by exploiting adjunctions that exist already at the level of posets.

Before we explain what an excisable poset is, recall first that for a fixed category
{\mathcal{T}}
, a
parametrised category
resp.
functor
is an object resp. morphism in
\func({\mathcal{T}}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Cat})
. This is the basic object in Barwick–Dotto–Glasman–Nardin–Shah’s theory of parametrised category theory
[
1
,
2
,
29
,
28
,
25
,
26
]
. A
parametrised poset
2
2

2

The consideration of such structured posets is not new: in the equivariant context, this has been explored for example in
[
31
,
32
,
18
,
23
]
and also in
[
7
,
8
]
for their equivariant calculus.
is then simply an object in the full subcategory
\func({\mathcal{T}}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Poset})\subseteq\func({\mathcal{T}}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Cat})
, and we say that it is
complementable
(cf.
Definition

2.1.1
) if it is distributive and for every object
x
, there exists another object
x^{c}
with
x\vee x^{c}=\mathbbm{1}
where
\mathbbm{1}
is the final object of the poset. Examples of complementable posets include those which are fibrewise given by cubes of various dimensions with (co)product–preserving transition functors.

Definition 1.0.1

(cf.
Definition

2.1.11
)
.

An
excisable poset
is the datum of a complementable parametrised poset
\underline{{L}}
together with a nonempty full subposet
\sigma\colon\breve{\underline{{L}}}\subseteq\underline{{L}}
which is downward closed, i.e. for all
u\in\mathcal{T}
, if
x\rightarrow y
is a morphism in
{L}(u)
where
y\in\breve{{L}}(u)
, then
x
is also in
\breve{{L}}(u)
.

Definition 1.0.2
.

Let
\sigma\colon\breve{\underline{{L}}}\subseteq\underline{{L}}
be an excisable poset and
F\colon\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
a parametrised functor. We say that
F
is
\sigma
–excisive
if it sends
\underline{{L}}
–indexed diagrams in
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
left Kan extended from
\breve{\underline{{L}}}
to a limit diagram in
\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
.

In the unparametrised context, these definitions recover Goodwillie’s notion of
n
–excisiveness by using the excisable poset given by the inclusion of subsets of size at most 1 in the
(n+1)
–cube.

A basic insight of the present work is that the simple
Definition

1.0.1
affords a sufficiently rich environment to support the trappings of a theory of excisiveness while simultaneously being easy to work with. The complementability hypothesis plays a key role in allowing us to separate out orthogonal directions in the poset by taking the product with an object
x\wedge-
(see
Lemma

2.1.8
for this procedure). What is more, since
x\wedge-
is an idempotent procedure, extracting these orthogonal directions yield a Bousfield localisation and colocalisation on the poset, see
Corollary

2.1.9
.Among other things, this allows us to generalise many of the proof steps due to Goodwillie (and in part, Rezk
[
27
]
) very smoothly. They will generally be specific instances of formal manipulations of left and right Kan extensions along adjunctions. It also allows for a formal definition of the faces of an excisable poset, see
Section

2.2
. This will be crucial in relating the different levels of excisiveness.

In particular, we generalise Goodwillie’s formula for excisive approximations to our setting. In more detail, we prove that under some presentability hypotheses, the inclusion
\mathrm{Exc}^{n}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})\subseteq\func({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
of
n
–excisive functors into all functors admit a left adjoint
P_{n}
for adjoint functor theorem reasons. However, what is remarkable is that, under a “differentiability” hypothesis (cf.
[
20
, Def. 6.1.1.6]
) on
{\mathcal{D}}
, Goodwillie’s methods provides us an explicit formula for
{P}_{n}
which is indispensable for both practical and theoretical purposes. For example, we have the formula

{P}_{1}F\simeq\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{n}\Omega^{n}F\Sigma^{n}.

Now, for an excisable poset
\sigma
, we give a similar explicit construction for
{P}_{\sigma}
(cf.
Construction

2.3.7
), and using a straightforward generalisation of the definition of differentiability to our context, our first main result may thus be stated as follows:

Theorem A

(Excisive approximations, cf.
Theorem

2.3.12
)
.

Let
\sigma\colon\breve{\underline{{L}}}\subseteq\underline{{L}}
be an excisable poset. Let
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}},\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{{\mathcal{T}}}
where
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
has
\sigma
–left Kan extensions and a final object and
\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
is
\sigma
–differentiable. Then the fully faithful inclusion
\underline{\mathrm{Exc}}^{\sigma}(\underline{{\mathcal{C}}},\underline{{\mathcal{D}}})\subseteq\underline{\func}(\underline{{\mathcal{C}}},\underline{{\mathcal{D}}})
admits a left adjoint given by
{P}_{\sigma}
.

As mentioned above, it is the specific formula for
{P}_{\sigma}
and not its mere existence that is the real content of the theorem. This is a point which we will crucially exploit to obtain our
Theorem

B
.

Cubes, spheres, and semiadditivity

Having set up some basic theory, one is then led to ask if this relates to or solves any problem that a priori has nothing to do with parametrised functor calculus. This is perhaps the light in which our second set of main results should be viewed, the context of which we explain now.

The following is one of the classical foundational results in equivariant stable homotopy theory, first proved by Wirthmüller
3
3

3

Even though Wirthmüller proved this for all compact Lie groups, we only give the case of finite groups since it is easier to state and is the only level of generality that is pertinent to this article.

[
33
]
at the level of homology theories.

Theorem 1.0.3
.

Let
G
be a finite group and
S^{\rho_{G}}
the regular representation sphere. Then writing
\mathcal{S}_{G,*}\coloneqq\func(\mathcal{O}_{G}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathcal{S}_{*})
for the category of pointed genuine
G
–spaces, the orbits
G/H_{+}\in\mathcal{S}_{G,*}
become self–dual in
\mathrm{Sp}_{G}\coloneqq\mathcal{S}_{G,*}[(S^{\rho_{G}})^{-1}]
.

This self–duality in
\mathrm{Sp}_{G}
is of fundamental importance in the methods of equivariant stable homotopy theory. In fact, such self–duality admits a generalisation in the form of
parametrised semiadditivity
due to Nardin
[
25
,
26
]
in any context parametrised over so–called “atomic orbital” base categories, see
loc. cit.
Using this, Nardin also defined the notion of
parametrised stability
for parametrised categories as being fibrewise stable and satisfying the aforementioned semiadditivity.

However, the relationship between representation spheres and the orbits turns out to be quite mysterious, at least from the categorical point of view. Indeed, the standard proof for
Theorem

1.0.3
uses equivariant smooth manifold theory via the Pontryagin–Thom collapse map to produce the duality datum witnessing the orbits as being self–dual. This approach is unfortunately not amenable to generalisation to the abstract parametrised setting where any kind of geometric argument is often not at our disposal. Moreover, unlike in the nonequivariant context where stability on a pointed category with sufficient colimits is tantamount to the suspension functor being an equivalence, Nardin’s definition of parametrised stability seems to “separate out” two distinct conditions. In light of this, one is then naturally led to ask the following:

Question 1.0.4
.

Is there an alternative characterisation of parametrised stability in terms of the invertibility of some appropriate spheres? If so, what should these spheres be?

To proceed, we turn to Dotto–Moi for guidance: in their
[
7
, Thm. 3.26]
,
Theorem

1.0.3
was proved essentially by way of a precursor to equivariant Goodwillie calculus, which was later fleshed out into a full theory by Dotto in
[
8
]
. Inspired by their methods, we specialise the axiomatic theory of excision above to a special family of excisable posets given by the
singleton inclusions
in
parametrised cubes
as introduced in
[
16
, §3]
(cf.
Construction

3.1.7
) whose existence is a special feature of base categories that are atomic orbital. Here, by a parametrised cube, we simply mean an indexed product of
\Delta^{1}
(cf.
Definition

3.1.3
). The role of these diagrams, facilitated by the key cubical inputs of
3.1.14
and
3.4.13
, is that they encode parametrised semiadditivity in the form of parametrised cubes, which in turn are primed for calculus–theoretic manipulations. We term the excisiveness with respect to these excisable posets as
singleton excision.

On the other hand, one can consider the excisable poset structure given by these parametrised cubes and the subposet away from the final object. Using this, we may construct certain
parametrised spheres
(cf.
Section

3.2
), supplying a replacement of the regular representation sphere in this general context. Excisiveness against such excisable posets are termed, correspondingly, as
spherical excision.
Furthermore, we say that a pointed parametrised category
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
is
spherically invertible
(cf.
Definition

3.2.4
) if the suspension functors along all parametrised cubes is an equivalence. By comparing singleton and spherical excisions, we then employ a “length induction” and use
Theorem

A
to provide the following result:

Theorem B

(cf.
Theorem

3.4.16
)
.

Suppose
{\mathcal{T}}
is a locally short atomic orbital category (cf.
Definition

3.4.9
). Let
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
be a pointed
{\mathcal{T}}
–category which is parametrised bicomplete. If
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
is spherically invertible, then it is
\mathcal{T}
–semiadditive.

In fact, the theorem is proved using the weaker hypothesis of
spherical faithfulness
(
Definition

3.2.4
), and to lend a convincing scope to the result, we supply plenty of examples of locally short atomic orbital categories in
Example

3.4.11
. This theorem may be viewed as a vast generalisation of the classical result above (and thus also of
[
7
, Thm. 3.26]
). Perhaps more philosophically, it shows that such results are not endemic just to the equivariant setting for finite groups and that they have a distinctly algebraic or categorical flavour independent of any geometric arguments. It bears mentioning, however, that our framework and results do
not
apply to equivariance with respect to general compact Lie groups. Currently it seems that there is something irreducibly geometric at this level of generality. In this sense,
Theorem

B
may be seen as a continuation in the development of our understanding of atomic orbital categories.

Under some additional hypotheses, we use
Theorem

B
to give a new characterisation of parametrised stability, providing an answer to
Question

1.0.4
.

Theorem C

(cf.
Theorem

3.4.17
)
.

Suppose
{\mathcal{T}}
is a locally short atomic orbital category such that
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}

\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}

admits a

-distributive symmetric monoidal structure. Let
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
be a pointed parametrised presentable

is parametrised stable if and only if it is spherically invertible.

In the statement of the theorem we must suppose that

By another application of the formula of excisive approximation furnished by
Theorem

A
, we also complement the preceding theorem by showing that, in some sense, there is just one notion of parametrised linearity in the stable setting.

Theorem D

(Precise version in
Theorem

3.4.19
)
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a locally short atomic orbital category,
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
a pointed parametrised bicomplete category,
\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
a spherically invertible parametrised presentable category, and
F\colon\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
a reduced and filtered colimit preserving functor. Then
F
preserving all parametrised colimits is equivalent to
F
being spherically excisive, and this is in turn equivalent to
F
being singleton excisive.

Structural overview

We develop the foundations of parametrised excisiveness in
Section

2
by formulating the notion of an excisable poset and proving the fundamental
Theorem

A
. In the next
Section

3
, we specialise the preceding theory to atomic orbital base categories to prove
Theorem

B
and
Theorem

C
by introducing the parametrised spheres. In
Appendix

A
, we record a parametrised enhancement of the decomposition results of
[
17
]
.

1.1
Prerequisites, notations, and conventions

This article is written in the formalism of parametrised higher categories as introduced and developed in
[
1
,
2
,
25
,
24
,
29
,
28
]
. For a convenient one–stop reference on this, see also for example the survey given in
[
15
, §2-4]
. Most of our notations will be based on this latter source. Since they will play such a prevalent role, we highlight the following notations so as to prevent any ambiguity.

Let
{\mathcal{T}}
be a base category. We shall write
\mathrm{Cat}_{{\mathcal{T}}}
for the category
\func({\mathcal{T}}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Cat})
of
{\mathcal{T}}
–categories and
\underline{\mathrm{Cat}}
for the
{\mathcal{T}}
–category of
{\mathcal{T}}
–categories. For a morphism
w\colon W\rightarrow V
in
\mathrm{Fin}_{{\mathcal{T}}}
, we write the associated induction–restriction–coinduction adjunctions as
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Cat}_{{\mathcal{T}}_{/V}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Cat}_{{\mathcal{T}}_{/W}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{*}}

For a
{\mathcal{T}}
–category
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{{\mathcal{T}}}
and an object
V\in\mathrm{Fin}_{{\mathcal{T}}}
, we write
V^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
for the associated
{\mathcal{T}}_{/V}
–category obtained by restriction along the functor
{\mathcal{T}}_{/V}\rightarrow{\mathcal{T}}
.

Moreover, letting
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
be a
{\mathcal{T}}
–category, by an object
x\in\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
, we shall mean an implicit choice of
V\in{\mathcal{T}}
and an object
x
in the nonparametrised category
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}(V)\simeq(V^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})(V)\in\mathrm{Cat}
given by evaluating
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
at
V\in{\mathcal{T}}
. As such, when we associate to such an object a functor, for example, the product functor
x\times-\colon\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
, we shall mean that there is an implicit basechange to
{\mathcal{T}}_{/V}
-categories, so that the functor is really the functor
x\times-\colon V^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\rightarrow V^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
. We have adopted this convention so as not to clutter the statements with too many symbols.

2
Parametrised excision for posets

We introduce and prove basic properties of the notions of excisable posets in
Section

2.1
and of their associated faces in
Section

2.2
; we then apply these preliminaries in
Section

2.3
to develop the concept of parametrised excision and prove
Theorem

A
. To begin, let us recall the notations an conventions from
Section

1.1
.

2.1
Excisable posets

We begin by defining some terms and recording some easy observations on posets.

Definition 2.1.1
.

A
parametrised poset
is an object in
\mathrm{Poset}_{\mathcal{T}}\coloneqq\func(\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Poset})\subseteq\mathrm{Cat}_{\mathcal{T}}
.

•

A parametrised poset is said to be
distributive
if it admits parametrised fibrewise
4
4

4

That is, the
{\mathcal{T}}
–category admits finite (co)products levelwise and the restriction functors between different levels preserve these.
finite products and coproducts which we write as
\wedge
and
\vee
respectively, and such that the canonical map
(a\wedge c)\vee(b\wedge c)\rightarrow(a\vee b)\wedge c
is an equivalence. We will write
\varnothing
and
\mathbbm{1}
for the initial and final objects, respectively.

•

If furthermore, for every object
a
, there exists another object
a^{c}
such that
a\vee a^{c}\simeq\mathbbm{1}
and
a\wedge a^{c}\simeq\varnothing
, then we say that the parametrised poset is
complementable
.

When the context is clear, we will often omit to mention the adjective “parametrised” and just refer to these objects as posets.

Remark 2.1.2
.

For simplicity we have phrased the definition above for parametrised categories over a small category
{\mathcal{T}}
. However, all the results in this section generalise immediately to the context of internal categories in any topos
\mathcal{B}
in the sense of
[
22
,
21
]
.

Remark 2.1.3
.

In posets, since the space of morphisms between objects are either empty or contractible, we see that coproducts also compute pushouts, and similarly, products compute pullbacks. Hence, for example when
S
is a finite set, in the poset of subsets
\mathrm{Pos}(S)
, coproducts are given by unions of subsets and products are given by intersections of subsets.

Remark 2.1.4
.

For posets, we always have that
x\wedge\varnothing=\varnothing
since the existence of the map
x\wedge\varnothing\rightarrow\varnothing
ensures that the mapping space out of
x\wedge\varnothing
is nonempty, and so contractible.

Lemma 2.1.5

(Idempotence of poset (co)products)
.

Let
\underline{{L}}\in\mathrm{Poset}_{\mathcal{T}}
. Then for any
x\in\underline{{L}}
, the fibrewise product
x\wedge x
and fibrewise coproduct
x\vee x
exist, and they are given by
x\wedge x\simeq x
and
x\vee x\simeq x
.

Proof.

We only do the case of coproducts, since the other is dual. Let
y\in\underline{{L}}
. If the coproduct
x\vee x
exists, then it must participate in the equivalences

\map_{\underline{{L}}}(x\vee x,y)\simeq\map_{\underline{{L}}}(x,y)\times\map_{\underline{{L}}}(x,y)\simeq\map_{\underline{{L}}}(x,y)

where the second equivalence is since
\varnothing\times\varnothing\simeq\varnothing
and
\ast\times\ast\simeq\ast
in spaces. Thus, we see that
x
satisfies the universal property of the coproduct.
∎

Lemma 2.1.6

(Saturation of (co)products)
.

Let
\underline{{L}}\in\mathrm{Poset}_{\mathcal{T}}
. Then for any
x,a\in\underline{{L}}
,
x\vee(x\wedge a)\simeq x
and
x\wedge(x\vee a)\simeq x
, if these (co)products exist.

Proof.

We only do the first case, since the second is dual. Let
y\in\underline{{L}}
and consider

\map_{\underline{{L}}}(x\vee(x\wedge a),y)\simeq\map_{\underline{{L}}}(x,y)\times\map_{\underline{{L}}}(x\wedge a,y)\longrightarrow\map_{\underline{{L}}}(x,y).

We argue that this map is an equivalence. If
\map_{\underline{{L}}}(x,y)\simeq\varnothing
, then both sides will be
\varnothing
in spaces, and so is an equivalence in this case. If
\map_{\underline{{L}}}\simeq\ast
, then there exists a map
x\rightarrow y
, and so by precomposition with
x\wedge a\rightarrow x
, we also see that
\map_{\underline{{L}}}(x\wedge a,y)\simeq\ast
. Thus, the map of interest is an equivalence again.
∎

We next work out several elementary properties of complementable posets before coming to the definition of an excisable poset, which will play a central role throughout the article. The complementability assumption will allow us to decompose our posets as a product of two smaller complementable posets in very pleasant ways.

Construction 2.1.7

(Smashing subposet)
.

Let
\underline{{L}}
be a complementable parametrised poset and
x\in\underline{{L}}
. Write
\underline{{L}}_{x}
for the full subcategory of
\underline{{L}}
spanned by the essential image of the idempotent functor
x\wedge-\colon\underline{{L}}\rightarrow\underline{{L}}
from
Lemma

2.1.5
. The poset
\underline{{L}}_{x}
clearly has initial object
\varnothing
since
x\wedge\varnothing\simeq\varnothing
, and by
Lemma

2.1.6
, it has a final object given by
x
. Furthermore, by distributivity, we also see that
\underline{{L}}_{x}\subseteq\underline{{L}}
is closed under nontrivial finite (co)products, and it is immediate to check that
\underline{{L}}_{x}
is again distributive. Hence,
\underline{{L}}_{x}
is another complementable parametrised poset. Moreover, it is also immediate to see that
\underline{{L}}_{x}\subseteq\underline{{L}}
is downward–closed.

Lemma 2.1.8

(Complement decompositions)
.

Let
\underline{{L}}
be a complementable parametrised poset and
x\in\underline{{L}}
. Then the functors
(x\wedge-)\times(x^{c}\wedge-)\colon\underline{{L}}\rightarrow\underline{{L}}_{x}\times\underline{{L}}_{x^{c}}
and
-\vee-\colon\underline{{L}}_{x}\times\underline{{L}}_{x^{c}}\rightarrow\underline{{L}}
are inverse equivalences to each other.

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of the fact that for any
a,b,c\in\underline{{L}}
, the natural maps

(x\wedge a)\vee(x^{c}\wedge a)\xlongrightarrow{\simeq}(x\vee x^{c})\wedge a\simeq\mathbbm{1}\wedge a\simeq a

x\wedge b\xlongrightarrow{\simeq}x\wedge(x\wedge b)\xlongrightarrow{\simeq}x\wedge((x\wedge b)\vee(x^{c}\wedge c))

x^{c}\wedge c\xlongrightarrow{\simeq}x^{c}\wedge(x^{c}\wedge c)\xlongrightarrow{\simeq}x^{c}\wedge((x\wedge b)\vee(x^{c}\wedge c))

are equivalences.
∎

Corollary 2.1.9

(Smashing (co)localisations)
.

Let
\underline{{L}}
be a complementable parametrised poset and
x\in\underline{{L}}
. Then we have the Bousfield (co)localisations
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{L}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{L}}_{x}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(x\wedge-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\varnothing\vee-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(x^{c}\vee-)}

Proof.

Since
\underline{{L}}_{x^{c}}
has an initial and final object by
Construction

2.1.7
, we have a Bousfield (co)localisation
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{L}}_{x^{c}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\ast}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varnothing}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x^{c}}

Thus by applying
\underline{{L}}_{x}\times-
to this Bousfield (co)localisation and using the decomposition from
Lemma

2.1.8
, we obtain the desired adjunctions.
∎

Lemma 2.1.10
.

Suppose
\underline{{L}}
is a complementable parametrised poset and
x\in\underline{{L}}
. Then the functor
x\vee-\colon\underline{{L}}\rightarrow\underline{{L}}
admits a left adjoint given by
x^{c}\wedge-
.

Proof.

Note that the functor
x\vee-\colon\underline{{L}}\rightarrow\underline{{L}}
is identified with the composite
\underline{{L}}\xrightarrow{x^{c}\wedge-}\underline{{L}}_{x^{c}}\xrightarrow{x\vee-}\underline{{L}}
. Thus, by
Corollary

2.1.9
, this admits a left adjoint given by
\underline{{L}}\xrightarrow{x^{c}\wedge-}\underline{{L}}_{x^{c}}\xrightarrow{\varnothing\vee-}\underline{{L}}
, which is identified with
x^{c}\wedge-
.
∎

We are now ready to define and elaborate on the properties of an excisable poset, which is supposed to axiomatise the notion of the star of an
(n+1)
–cube (i.e. the subposet of
(\Delta^{1})^{\times n}
, thought of as the poset of subsets of the set with
n+1
elements, consisting of those subsets of size at most 1) that appears in Goodwillie’s definition of strongly cocartesian cubes.

Definition 2.1.11
.

An
excisable poset
is the datum of a complementable poset
\underline{{L}}\in\mathrm{Poset}_{\mathcal{T}}
together with a nonempty full subposet
\sigma\colon\breve{\underline{{L}}}\subseteq\underline{{L}}
which is downward closed, i.e. for all
u\in\mathcal{T}
, if
x\rightarrow y
is a morphism in
{L}(u)
where
y\in\breve{{L}}(u)
, then
x
is also in
\breve{{L}}(u)
.

Observation 2.1.12
.

Note in particular that
\breve{\underline{{L}}}
also contains an initial object.

Lemma 2.1.13
.

Let
\underline{{L}}
be a complementable parametrised poset,
x\in\underline{{L}}
, and
\breve{{L}}\subseteq\underline{{L}}
a downward–closed subposet. Then writing
\breve{\underline{{L}}}_{x}\subseteq\underline{{L}}_{x}
for the image of the composite
\breve{\underline{{L}}}\subseteq\underline{{L}}\xrightarrow{(x\wedge-)}\underline{{L}}_{x}
, we have that
\breve{\underline{{L}}}_{x}\subseteq\underline{{L}}_{x}
is also downward–closed.

Proof.

Let
u\in\breve{\underline{{L}}}_{x}
, so that
u=x\wedge m
for some
m\in\breve{\underline{{L}}}
, and suppose we have a morphism
v\rightarrow u
in
\underline{{L}}_{x}
. Thus, we have the morphism
v\rightarrow u=x\wedge m\rightarrow m
in
\underline{{L}}
with
m\in\breve{\underline{{L}}}
. Hence, since
\breve{\underline{{L}}}\subseteq\underline{{L}}
is downward–closed, we also have that
v\in\breve{\underline{{L}}}
. Furthermore, since we have the morphism
x^{c}\wedge v\rightarrow x^{c}\wedge x\wedge m=\varnothing
in
\underline{{L}}
, we see that
x^{c}\wedge v=\varnothing
by
Remark

2.1.4
, and so
x\wedge v\rightarrow v
is an equivalence. All in all, we see that
v\in\breve{\underline{{L}}}_{x}
as claimed.
∎

To end this subsection, we introduce the analogues of strong cocartesianness and cartesianness in our setup following
[
30
]
, and establish some technical properties needed in the proof of
Theorem

A
.

Notation 2.1.14
.

Given a complementable parametrised poset
\underline{{L}}
, we let
\mathring{\sigma}\colon\underline{\mathring{{L}}}\subseteq\underline{{L}}
be the full subposet away from the initial object. Moreover, given an excisable poset
\sigma
, we write
\widehat{\sigma}\colon\underline{\ast}\hookrightarrow\breve{\underline{{L}}}
for the inclusion of the initial object.

Terminology 2.1.15
.

Let
f\colon\underline{I}\rightarrow\underline{J}
be a functor and
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{{\mathcal{T}}}
. We say that
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
is
f
–left–extensible (resp.
f
–right–extensible)
if the left Kan extensions (resp. right Kan extensions) along
f
exist in
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
.

Definition 2.1.16
.

An object in
\underline{\func}(\underline{{L}},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})
is said to be
\sigma
–cocartesian
if it is in the image of the fully faithful functor
\sigma_{!}\colon\underline{\func}(\breve{\underline{{L}}},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})\hookrightarrow\underline{\func}({\underline{{L}}},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})
. It is said to be
cartesian
if it is in the image of the fully faithful functor
\mathring{\sigma}_{*}\colon\underline{\func}(\underline{\mathring{{L}}},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})\hookrightarrow\underline{\func}({\underline{{L}}},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})
. We write
\underline{\mathrm{Fun}}^{{\sigma}_{!}}(\underline{{L}},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})
and
\underline{\mathrm{Fun}}^{\mathrm{cart}}(\underline{{L}},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})
for the full subcategories of
\sigma
–cocartesian and cartesian functors, respectively.

Proposition 2.1.17
.

Let
\sigma
be an excisable poset,
x\in\underline{{L}}
, and
D\in\underline{\mathrm{Fun}}^{{\sigma}_{!}}(\underline{{L}},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})
. Then the functor
D\circ(x\vee-)\colon\underline{{L}}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
is also
\sigma
–cocartesian.

Proof.

We need to show that the adjunction counit
\sigma_{!}\sigma^{*}(x\vee-)^{*}D\rightarrow(x\vee-)^{*}D
is an equivalence. Recall that we had an adjunction
x^{c}\wedge-\colon\underline{{L}}\rightleftharpoons\underline{{L}}:x\vee-
from
Lemma

2.1.10
, and so in particular, we have
(x\vee-)^{*}\simeq(x^{c}\wedge-)_{!}
. Next, we notice that there is a dashed factorisation in the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{L}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{L}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\breve{\underline{{L}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\breve{\underline{{L}}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x^{c}\wedge-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x^{c}\wedge-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}

This is because for
a\in\breve{\underline{{L}}}
, the adjunction counit
x^{c}\wedge(x\vee a)\rightarrow a
may be precomposed with
x^{c}\wedge a\rightarrow x^{c}\wedge(x\vee a)
to give a map
x^{c}\wedge a\rightarrow a
. Hence, since
\breve{\underline{{L}}}
was downward–closed,
x^{c}\wedge a
is also in
\breve{\underline{{L}}}
. Since
D
was
\sigma
–cocartesian, we may write
D=\sigma_{!}\overline{D}
. Now, we consider the computation:

\begin{split}\sigma_{!}\sigma^{*}(x\vee-)^{*}D&\simeq\sigma_{!}\sigma^{*}(x\vee-)^{*}\sigma_{!}\overline{D}\\
&\simeq\sigma_{!}\sigma^{*}(x^{c}\wedge-)_{!}\sigma_{!}\overline{D}\\
&\simeq\sigma_{!}\sigma^{*}\sigma_{!}(x^{c}\wedge-)_{!}\overline{D}\\
&\simeq\sigma_{!}(x^{c}\wedge-)_{!}\overline{D}\\
&\simeq(x^{c}\wedge-)_{!}\sigma_{!}\overline{D}\\
&\simeq(x\vee-)^{*}\sigma_{!}\overline{D}=(x\vee-)^{*}D\end{split}

where the fourth equivalence used that
\sigma^{*}\sigma_{!}\simeq\mathrm{id}
by virtue of fully faithfulness.
∎

We now work towards the cartesian analogue of the result above.

Lemma 2.1.18
.

Let
\underline{P},\underline{I},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{\mathcal{T}}
where
\underline{P}
and
\underline{I}
have initial objects,
\underline{I}
has a final object not equivalent to the initial object, and
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
has the appropriate Kan extensions. Then for any
F\colon\underline{P}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
, the diagram
F\circ\pi\colon\underline{P}\times\underline{I}\xrightarrow{\pi}\underline{P}\xrightarrow{F}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
is cartesian.

Proof.

Since
\underline{I}
has a final object, the map
\pi
has a right adjoint
r
. Moreover because the final object is different then initial object, the projection
\overline{\pi}\colon(\underline{P}\times\underline{I})\backslash\varnothing\rightarrow\underline{P}
also has a right adjoint
\overline{r}
. It is easy to see that we have the solid commuting diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{P}\times\underline{I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\underline{P}\times\underline{I})\backslash\varnothing}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\pi}}

Note that
r^{*}\dashv\pi^{*}
and
\overline{r}^{*}\dashv\overline{\pi}^{*}
. Therefore we compute that

\pi^{*}F\simeq r_{*}F\simeq i_{*}\overline{r}_{*}F\simeq i_{*}\overline{\pi}^{*}F,

proving the claim.
∎

Proposition 2.1.19
.

Let
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{\mathcal{T}}
,
\sigma
an excisable poset, and
x\neq\varnothing\in\underline{{L}}
. Then for any
D\colon\underline{{L}}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
, the diagram
D\circ(x\vee-)
is cartesian.

Proof.

Using
Lemma

2.1.8
, we see that the functor
(x\vee-)\colon\underline{{L}}\rightarrow\underline{{L}}
factors as

(x\vee-)\colon\underline{{L}}\simeq\underline{{L}}_{x^{c}}\times\underline{{L}}_{x}\xlongrightarrow{\pi}\underline{{L}}_{x^{c}}\xlongrightarrow{(x\vee-)}\underline{{L}}

Since
x\neq\varnothing
,
\underline{{L}}_{x}
has initial and final objects which are not equivalent. Thus, we may apply
Lemma

2.1.18
to see that postcomposing with
F\colon\underline{{L}}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
yields a cartesian diagram, as wanted.
∎

2.2
Face decompositions

We now axiomatise the important manoeuvre of decomposing cubes into its faces. We first work towards formulating and proving a generalisation of the classical statement (cf. for instance
[
20
, Prop. 6.1.1.13]
) that if a cube is strongly cocartesian, then so are all its faces. The materials here are needed in order to relate different notions of excisiveness later across different excisable posets, but are not needed for the proof of
Theorem

A
. Since the proofs are rather technical, the reader should feel free to skip this subsection on first reading.

To define the faces of a complementable parametrised poset we require the following definition.

Definition 2.2.1
.

Given a complementable parametrised poset, we say
a,d\in\underline{L}
are disjoint if
a\wedge d=\varnothing
. We say three elements
a,d,z\in\underline{{L}}
are
a decomposition of
\underline{{L}}
if
a,d
and
z
are pairwise disjoint and
a\vee d\vee z=\mathbbm{1}
.

Remark 2.2.2
.

Note that given two disjoint elements
a,d\in\underline{{L}}
, there is a unique
z\in\underline{{L}}
such that
a,d,z
is a decomposition of
\underline{L}
. In fact,
z
must be equal to
(a\vee d)^{c}
.

Construction 2.2.3

(Face posets along triple decompositions)
.

Let
\underline{{L}}
be a complementable parametrised poset and let
a,d,z\in\underline{{L}}
be a decomposition of
\underline{{L}}
. Thus recalling the notations from
Construction

2.1.7
and the Bousfield (co)localisation from
Corollary

2.1.9
, we obtain the Bousfield (co)localisations
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{L}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{L}}_{a\vee d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{L}}_{a\vee d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{L}}_{a}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cap_{z}\coloneqq((a\vee d)\wedge-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{z}\coloneqq(\varnothing\vee-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega_{z}\coloneqq(z\vee-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cap\coloneqq(a\wedge-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\coloneqq(\varnothing\vee-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\coloneqq(d\vee-)}

The
a
–face associated to the decomposition
a,d,z
is defined to be the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi\coloneqq\Phi_{d}\coloneqq\Phi_{a,d,z}\colon\underline{{L}}_{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{L}}_{a\vee d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{z}}

(1)

which is a fully faithful functor. This generalises
[
20
, Def. 6.1.1.12]
to our setup.

Futhermore, if we have an excisable structure on the original poset, then there is also an induced excisable structure on the face posets as the following point explains.

Construction 2.2.4

(Excisable structure on face posets)
.

Suppose we have the structure of an excisable poset
\sigma\colon\breve{\underline{{L}}}\subseteq\underline{{L}}
and suppose
a,d,z
are a decomposition of
\underline{{L}}
. Then by
2.1.13
and
2.1.7
, we also obtain the structure of an induced excisable poset
\sigma_{a}\colon\breve{\underline{{L}}}_{a}\subseteq\underline{{L}}
on
\underline{{L}}_{a}
by taking
\breve{\underline{{L}}}_{a}
to be the image of
\breve{\underline{{L}}}\subseteq\underline{{L}}\xrightarrow{\cap\circ\cap_{z}}\underline{{L}}_{a}
. Now consider the pullbacks

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathcal{I}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{L}}_{a\vee d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathcal{J}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{L}}_{a\vee d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\breve{\underline{{L}}}_{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{L}}_{a}.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\breve{\underline{{L}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\cap}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cap}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\Omega}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}

(2)

For the left square in
Eq.

2
, since the arrow
\cap
admits a fully faithful right adjoint, by standard abstract nonsense, so does the functor
\overline{\cap}
, which we call
\overline{\Omega}
; by construction, this also satisfies that the canonical map
j\circ\overline{\Omega}\rightarrow\Omega\circ\sigma_{a}
is an equivalence. For the right square, since both
\sigma\colon\breve{\underline{{L}}}\subseteq\underline{{L}}
and
A_{z}\colon\underline{{L}}_{a\vee d}\subseteq\underline{{L}}
are downward–closed, so is the inclusion
\tau\colon\underline{\mathcal{J}}\subseteq\underline{{L}}_{a\vee d}
, and so it provides an excisable poset structure on
\underline{{L}}_{a\vee d}
.

Moreover, since by definition
\breve{\underline{{L}}}\subseteq\underline{{L}}\xrightarrow{\cap\circ\cap_{z}}\underline{{L}}_{a}
lands in
\breve{\underline{{L}}}_{a}
, we get a square
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathcal{J}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{L}}_{a\vee d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\breve{\underline{{L}}}_{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{L}}_{a},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cap}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{a}}

whence
\tau\colon\underline{\mathcal{J}}\subseteq\underline{{L}}_{a\vee d}
factorises via an inclusion
k\colon\underline{\mathcal{J}}\hookrightarrow\underline{\mathcal{I}}
.

Proposition 2.2.5
.

Let
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
be a parametrised cocomplete category,
\sigma\colon\breve{\underline{{L}}}\subseteq\underline{{L}}
be an excisable poset, and
a\in\underline{{L}}
. Let
X\colon\underline{{L}}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
be a
\sigma
–cocartesian diagram. Then every
a
–face of
X
is
\sigma_{a}
–cocartesian.

Proof.

We borrow the notation from
Construction

2.2.4
. We fix the
a
-face
\Phi\colon\underline{{L}}_{a}\hookrightarrow\underline{{L}}
from
Eq.

1
associated to the decomposition
a,d,z
of
\underline{{L}}
. Our goal is to show that the functor
\Phi^{*}X\in\underline{\func}(\underline{{L}}_{a},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})
is
\sigma_{a}
-cocartesian, in other words that the map
\sigma_{a!}\sigma_{a}^{*}\Phi^{*}X\rightarrow\Phi^{*}X
is an equivalence.

First consider the right square in
Eq.

2
. A simple computation using the formula for pointwise Kan-extensions and the fact that
\underline{{L}}_{a\vee d}
is downward closed in
\underline{{L}}
shows that
Y\coloneqq A_{z}^{*}X\in\underline{\func}(\underline{{L}}_{a\vee d},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})
is
\tau
–cocartesian, i.e. the map
\tau_{!}\tau^{*}Y\rightarrow Y
is an equivalence. Now consider the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{L}}_{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{L}}_{a\vee d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\breve{\underline{{L}}}_{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathcal{I}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathcal{J}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{*}X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cap}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\Omega}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\cap}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}

coming from
Construction

2.2.4
, where the squares commute. The adjunctions induce adjunctions
\Omega^{*}\dashv\cap^{*}
and
\overline{\Omega}^{*}\dashv\overline{\cap}^{*}
on the respective functor categories valued in
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
, and so in particular we get
\sigma_{a!}\overline{\Omega}^{*}\simeq\sigma_{a!}\overline{\cap}_{!}\simeq\cap_{!}j_{!}\simeq{\Omega}^{*}j_{!}
. Moreover, we also have

j^{*}Y\simeq j^{*}\tau_{!}\tau^{*}Y\simeq j^{*}j_{!}k_{!}k^{*}j^{*}Y\simeq k_{!}k^{*}j^{*}Y
, using that
j
is fully faithful and that
Y
is
\tau
-cocartesian. Thus, putting everything together, we obtain an equivalence

\Phi^{*}X\simeq\Omega^{*}Y\simeq\Omega^{*}j_{!}j^{*}Y\simeq\sigma_{a!}\overline{\Omega}^{*}j^{*}Y\simeq\sigma_{a!}\sigma_{a}^{*}\Omega^{*}Y\simeq\sigma_{a!}\sigma_{a}^{*}\Phi^{*}X,

as was to be shown.
∎

Next, we shall prove a generalisation of the fact that if all the faces of a cube are cartesian, then so is the cube itself. We will require the following lemma.

Lemma 2.2.6
.

Let
\underline{{L}}
be a complementable parametrised poset and let
a,d,z\in\underline{{L}}
be a decomposition of
\underline{{L}}
. Then the face functor
\Phi=\Phi_{d}
from
Eq.

1
induces a Bousfield colocalisation
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{\prime}_{d}=d\vee(-)\colon\underline{{L}}_{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{L}}_{d/}\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu\cap^{\prime}\coloneqq a\wedge(-).}

Proof.

We need to show that
\mathchoice{\mbox{\uline{\phantom{$\displaystyle\map$}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.5pt}[0.0pt]{$\displaystyle\map$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip to0.0pt{\hskip-0.46193pt\raisebox{0.19133pt}[0.0pt]{$\displaystyle\map$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.35355pt\raisebox{0.35355pt}[0.0pt]{$\displaystyle\map$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.19133pt\raisebox{0.46193pt}[0.0pt]{$\displaystyle\map$}\hss}\mbox{$\displaystyle\map$}}}}{\mbox{\uline{\phantom{$\textstyle\map$}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.5pt}[0.0pt]{$\textstyle\map$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.19133pt\raisebox{0.46193pt}[0.0pt]{$\textstyle\map$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip to0.0pt{\hskip-0.35355pt\raisebox{-0.35355pt}[0.0pt]{$\textstyle\map$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.46193pt\raisebox{-0.19133pt}[0.0pt]{$\textstyle\map$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{$\textstyle\map$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.46193pt\raisebox{0.19133pt}[0.0pt]{$\textstyle\map$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.35355pt\raisebox{0.35355pt}[0.0pt]{$\textstyle\map$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.19133pt\raisebox{0.46193pt}[0.0pt]{$\textstyle\map$}\hss}\mbox{$\textstyle\map$}}}}{\mbox{\uline{\phantom{$\scriptstyle\map$}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip to0.0pt{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\map$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.46193pt\raisebox{0.19133pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\map$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.35355pt\raisebox{0.35355pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\map$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.19133pt\raisebox{0.46193pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\map$}\hss}\mbox{$\scriptstyle\map$}}}}{\mbox{\uline{\phantom{$\scriptscriptstyle\map$}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.5pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\map$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.19133pt\raisebox{0.46193pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\map$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.35355pt\raisebox{0.35355pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\map$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.46193pt\raisebox{0.19133pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\map$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip
