0.35355pt\raisebox{-0.35355pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\map$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.19133pt\raisebox{-0.46193pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\map$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-0.5pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\map$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.19133pt\raisebox{-0.46193pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\map$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.35355pt\raisebox{-0.35355pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\map$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.46193pt\raisebox{-0.19133pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\map$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\map$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.46193pt\raisebox{0.19133pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\map$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.35355pt\raisebox{0.35355pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\map$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.19133pt\raisebox{0.46193pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\map$}\hss}\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\map$}}}}_{\underline{{L}}_{a}}(x,a\wedge t)
for all
x\in\underline{{L}}_{a}
and
t\in\underline{{L}}_{d/}
. Note that there is a map
d\rightarrow t
and that
a\wedge x\rightarrow x
is an equivalence. Therefore we can compute that both sides (which are equivalent apriori to either
\underline{\ast}
or
\underline{\varnothing}
) are nonempty if and only if there is a map
x\rightarrow t
in
\underline{{L}}
, whence the desired equivalence.
∎

Proposition 2.2.7
.

Let
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
be a parametrised cocomplete category,
\underline{{L}}
be a complementable parametrised poset, and
a\in\underline{{L}}
. Let
X\colon\underline{{L}}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
be a diagram whose
a
–faces are all cartesian. Then
X
is itself cartesian.

Proof.

Recalling the notation
\mathring{\sigma}\colon\mathring{\underline{{L}}}\subseteq\underline{{L}}
for the inclusion away from the initial object, we need to show that the canonical map
X\rightarrow\mathring{\sigma}_{*}\mathring{\sigma}^{*}X
is an equivalence. We break up the problem into two steps, and for this, consider
\underline{\mathcal{M}}\subseteq\mathring{\underline{{L}}}
the subposet of all objects that intersect nontrivially with
a
, i.e. the pullback
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathcal{M}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathring{\underline{{L}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathring{\underline{{L}}}_{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{L}}_{a}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cap\circ\cap_{z}\circ\mathring{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathring{\sigma}_{a}}

Our first claim is that
\mathring{\sigma}^{*}X
is a right Kan extension of
m^{*}\mathring{\sigma}^{*}X
, i.e. the map
\mathring{\sigma}^{*}X\rightarrow m_{*}m^{*}\mathring{\sigma}^{*}X
is an equivalence. This may be checked for every parametrised point in
\mathring{\underline{{L}}}
. By passing to the appropriate restrictions if necessary, without loss of generality, suppose we have a point
d\in\mathring{\underline{{L}}}\backslash\underline{\mathcal{M}}
so that
a\wedge d=\varnothing
. Writing
z\coloneqq(a\vee d)^{c}
, we thus get the triple decomposition
\mathbbm{1}=a\vee d\vee z
and along with it, an
a
–face
\Phi_{d}=\Phi_{a,d,z}\colon\underline{{L}}_{a}\hookrightarrow\underline{{L}}
as in
Eq.

1
which sends
\varnothing
to
d\in\mathring{\underline{{L}}}
(in particular lands in
\mathring{\underline{{L}}}\subseteq\underline{{L}}
). Writing
\mathring{\Phi}_{d}\colon\underline{{L}}_{a}\hookrightarrow\underline{\mathring{{L}}}
for this induced map, we will thus show that

\mathring{\Phi}_{d}^{*}\mathring{\sigma}^{*}X\longrightarrow\mathring{\Phi}_{d}^{*}m_{*}m^{*}\mathring{\sigma}^{*}X

is an equivalence. To see this, consider the commuting diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathcal{M}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathring{\underline{{L}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathcal{M}}_{d/}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathring{\underline{{L}}}_{d/}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{L}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathring{\underline{{L}}_{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{L}}_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathring{\sigma}}

\lrcorner

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\nu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\ell}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathring{\overline{\sigma}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathring{\sigma}_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\varphi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{d}}

where all squares commute including the one involving the dashed maps, by construction of
\underline{\mathcal{M}}
, and the adjunctions are by
Lemma

2.2.6
. Observe that since the top square is a pullback involving fully faithful functors between posets, another computation using the formula for pointwise Kan extensions yields that the Beck–Chevalley map
\ell^{*}m_{*}\rightarrow\overline{m}_{*}\overline{\ell}^{*}
is an equivalence. Furthermore, we also have that
\overline{\nu}^{*}\dashv\overline{\varphi}^{*}
and
\nu^{*}\dashv\varphi^{*}
, so that
\overline{\varphi}^{*}\simeq\overline{\nu}_{*}
and
{\varphi}^{*}\simeq{\nu}_{*}
. Therefore, we may now compute:

\begin{split}\mathring{\Phi}_{d}^{*}m_{*}m^{*}\mathring{\sigma}^{*}X&\simeq\varphi^{*}\ell^{*}m_{*}m^{*}\mathring{\sigma}^{*}X\\
&\simeq{\varphi}^{*}\overline{m}_{*}\overline{\ell}^{*}m^{*}\mathring{\sigma}^{*}X\\
&\simeq{\varphi}^{*}\overline{m}_{*}\overline{m}^{*}\mathring{\overline{\sigma}}^{*}X\\
&\simeq{\nu}_{*}\overline{m}_{*}\overline{m}^{*}\mathring{\overline{\sigma}}^{*}X\\
&\simeq\mathring{\sigma}_{a*}\overline{{\nu}}_{*}\overline{m}^{*}\mathring{\overline{\sigma}}^{*}X\\
&\simeq\mathring{\sigma}_{a*}\overline{\varphi}^{*}\overline{m}^{*}\mathring{\overline{\sigma}}^{*}X\\
&\simeq\mathring{\sigma}_{a*}\mathring{\sigma}_{a}^{*}\Phi_{d}^{*}X\\
&\simeq\Phi_{d}^{*}X\\
&\simeq\mathring{\Phi}_{d}^{*}\mathring{\sigma}^{*}X\end{split}

as desired, where the penultimate equivalence is because the
a
–face
\Phi_{d}^{*}X
is cartesian.

For the second step, recall that ultimately we want to show that the canonical map
X\rightarrow\mathring{\sigma}_{*}\mathring{\sigma}^{*}X
is an equivalence. But since the inclusion
\mathring{\sigma}\colon\mathring{\underline{{L}}}\hookrightarrow\underline{{L}}
only adds the initial object
\varnothing
, it suffices to show that
\Phi_{\varnothing}^{*}X\rightarrow\Phi_{\varnothing}^{*}\mathring{\sigma}_{*}\mathring{\sigma}^{*}X
is an equivalence. In this case, we would need a similar diagram as in the previous step adjusted by the fact that the case
d=\varnothing
is special and slightly simpler:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathcal{M}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathring{\underline{{L}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{L}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathring{\underline{{L}}_{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{L}}_{a}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{{\nu}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathring{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathring{\sigma}_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\Phi}_{\varnothing}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi}_{\varnothing}}

Similarly as in the previous step, we may now compute:

\begin{split}\Phi_{\varnothing}^{*}\mathring{\sigma}_{*}\mathring{\sigma}^{*}X&\simeq\Phi_{\varnothing}^{*}\mathring{\sigma}_{*}m_{*}m^{*}\mathring{\sigma}^{*}X\\
&\simeq\nu_{*}\mathring{\sigma}_{*}m_{*}m^{*}\mathring{\sigma}^{*}X\\
&\simeq\mathring{\sigma}_{a*}\overline{{\nu}}_{*}m^{*}\mathring{\sigma}^{*}X\\
&\simeq\mathring{\sigma}_{a*}\overline{{\Phi}}_{\varnothing}^{*}m^{*}\mathring{\sigma}^{*}X\\
&\simeq\mathring{\sigma}_{a*}\mathring{\sigma}_{a}^{*}\Phi_{\varnothing}^{*}X\\
&\simeq\Phi_{\varnothing}^{*}X\end{split}

where the first equivalence is by the first step above, and the last equivalence is since the
a
–face
\Phi_{\varnothing}^{*}X
is cartesian. This completes the proof of the proposition.
∎

2.3
Excision

Armed with the requisite theory of posets, we are ready to formulate what excisiveness means in our context and work towards proving
Theorem

A
on the formula for excisive approximations. As already alluded to in the introduction, as in ordinary Goodwillie calculus, under presentability hypotheses, say, the mere existence of this approximation is always true. The real value of the theorem, which necessitates the work in this subsection, is that this approximation has an explicit formula given by
Construction

2.3.7
. This will play a crucial role in our proof of
Theorem

3.4.16
in
Section

3
.

Definition 2.3.1
.

Let
F\colon\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
be a functor where
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
is
\sigma
–left–extensible and
\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
is
\mathring{\sigma}
–right–extensible. We say that
F
is
\sigma
–excisive
if the diagram in
\mathrm{Cat}_{\mathcal{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\func}(\underline{{L}},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\func}(\underline{{L}},\underline{{\mathcal{D}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\func}(\breve{\underline{{L}}},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\func}(\underline{\mathring{{L}}},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathring{\sigma}_{*}}

admits the dashed factorisation. In other words, it sends
\sigma
–cocartesian diagrams to cartesian ones. We denote by
\underline{\mathrm{Exc}}^{\sigma}(\underline{{\mathcal{C}}},\underline{{\mathcal{D}}})
for the full subcategory of
\underline{\func}(\underline{{\mathcal{C}}},\underline{{\mathcal{D}}})
on the
\sigma
–excisive functors.

Remark 2.3.2
.

Since
\mathring{\sigma}_{*}\colon\underline{\func}(\underline{\mathring{{L}}},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})\rightarrow\underline{\func}(\underline{{L}},\underline{{\mathcal{D}}})
is fully faithful, it is really just a property that the dashed factorisation exists.

Remark 2.3.3
.

Note that in
Definition

2.3.1
we have required the factorisation to happen in
\mathrm{Cat}_{\mathcal{T}}
. Thus, this notion is stable under basechange, i.e. for all
u\in\mathcal{T}
, restriction to
\mathrm{Cat}_{\mathcal{T}_{/u}}
still yields a factorisation of
\mathcal{T}_{/u}
–categories.

In order to state the theorem, we will need one last piece of terminology.

Definition 2.3.4
.

Let
\sigma
be an excisable poset. A category
\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{\mathcal{T}}
is said to be
\sigma
–differentiable
if it admits sequential colimits and
\mathring{\underline{{L}}}
–indexed limits, and that these commute with each other.

We now begin to work on proving our first main result in earnest. We first collect some auxiliary constructions and their properties that will go into
Construction

2.3.7
. The next construction should of course be compared to
[
20
, Cons. 6.1.1.22]
.

Construction 2.3.5

(Pre–excisive approximation functor)
.

Suppose
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
has a final object. We construct a functor

{T}_{\sigma}\colon\underline{\func}(\underline{{\mathcal{C}}},\underline{{\mathcal{D}}})\longrightarrow\underline{\func}(\underline{{\mathcal{C}}},\underline{{\mathcal{D}}})

(3)

To this end, first consider the functor
{C}_{\sigma}
given by the composite of fully faithful functors

{C}_{\sigma}\colon\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\xhookrightarrow{\widehat{\sigma}_{*}}\underline{\func}(\breve{\underline{{L}}},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})\xhookrightarrow[\simeq]{\sigma_{!}}\underline{\mathrm{Fun}}^{{\sigma}_{!}}(\underline{{L}},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}).

The functor in
Eq.

3
is then defined as the composite

{T}_{\sigma}\colon\underline{\func}(\underline{{\mathcal{C}}},\underline{{\mathcal{D}}})\xlongrightarrow{(-)^{\underline{{L}}}}\underline{\func}(\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}^{\underline{{L}}},\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}^{\underline{{L}}})\xlongrightarrow{{C}_{\sigma}^{*}}\underline{\func}(\underline{{\mathcal{C}}},\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}^{\underline{{L}}})\xlongrightarrow{\widehat{\sigma}^{*}\mathring{\sigma}_{*}\mathring{\sigma}^{*}}\underline{\func}(\underline{{\mathcal{C}}},\underline{{\mathcal{D}}})

Observe that this admits a natural transformation

\theta\colon\mathrm{id}\rightarrow{T}_{\sigma}

(4)

from the identity functor induced by the adjunction unit
\eta\colon\mathrm{id}\rightarrow\mathring{\sigma}_{*}\mathring{\sigma}^{*}
. In more detail, for
F\in\underline{\func}(\underline{{\mathcal{C}}},\underline{{\mathcal{D}}})
, by virtue of the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}^{\underline{{L}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}^{\underline{{L}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{\mathcal{D}}},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C}_{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\sigma}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\underline{{L}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\sigma}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

the map
\theta_{F}\colon F\rightarrow{T}_{\sigma}F
is given by

\theta_{F}\colon F\simeq\widehat{\sigma}^{*}{C}_{\sigma}^{*}F^{\underline{{L}}}\xlongrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\widehat{\sigma}^{*}\eta_{{C}_{\sigma}^{*}}\hskip 8.19447pt}\widehat{\sigma}^{*}\mathring{\sigma}_{*}\mathring{\sigma}^{*}{C}_{\sigma}^{*}F^{\underline{{L}}}={T}_{\sigma}F.

Lemma 2.3.6
.

Suppose
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
is
\sigma
–extensible and has a final object. Then the functor
{C}_{\sigma}\colon\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\rightarrow\underline{\func}(\underline{{L}},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})
preserves
\sigma
–left Kan extensions and final objects.

Proof.

We first show that it preserves final objects. By construction, we know that
{C}_{\sigma}\simeq\sigma_{!}\circ\widehat{\sigma}_{*}
, and so since
\widehat{\sigma}_{*}
preserves limits, it suffices to argue that
\sigma_{!}\colon\underline{\func}(\breve{\underline{{L}}},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})\rightarrow\underline{\func}(\underline{{L}},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})
preserves final objects. Now, the final object in
\underline{\func}(\breve{\underline{{L}}},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})
is given by
\operatorname{const}_{\ast_{{\mathcal{C}}}}
. For every
\ell\in\underline{{L}}
, we know that

(\sigma_{!}\operatorname{const}_{\ast_{{\mathcal{C}}}})(\ell)\simeq\mathop{colim}\displaylimits\big((\sigma\downarrow\ell)\longrightarrow\breve{\underline{{L}}}\rightarrow\underline{\ast}\xlongrightarrow{{\ast_{{\mathcal{C}}}}}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\big)

Note that
\underline{\ast}
is a groupoid, and so the map
(\sigma\downarrow\ell)\rightarrow\underline{\ast}
factors through the localisation
|\sigma\downarrow\ell|
. Because localisations are colimit cofinal we may compute and
(\sigma_{!}\operatorname{const}_{\ast_{{\mathcal{C}}}})(\ell)
as a colimit over
|\sigma\downarrow\ell|
. However
|\sigma\downarrow\ell|\simeq\underline{\ast}
since
(\sigma\downarrow\ell)
has an initial object
\varnothing\rightarrow\ell
, and so we see that
(\sigma_{!}\operatorname{const}_{\ast_{{\mathcal{C}}}})(\ell)\simeq\ast_{{\mathcal{C}}}
, as was to be shown.

Finally, to see that
{C}_{\sigma}
preserves
\sigma
–left Kan extensions, we just have to show that the composite square in the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\func}(\underline{{L}},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\func}(\underline{{L}}\times\breve{\underline{{L}}},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\func}(\underline{{L}}\times\underline{{L}},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\func}(\breve{\underline{{L}}},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\func}(\breve{\underline{{L}}}\times\breve{\underline{{L}}},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\func}(\breve{\underline{{L}}}\times\underline{{L}},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{id}\times\widehat{\sigma})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{id}\times{\sigma})_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{id}\times\widehat{\sigma})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{id}\times{\sigma})_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\sigma\times\mathrm{id})_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\sigma\times\mathrm{id})_{!}}

commutes. However the right square clearly commutes, and so it suffices to show that Beck–Chevalley left square in the diagram commutes. But since
\widehat{\sigma}_{*}
is given by the final object at every point of
\breve{\underline{{L}}}
away from
\varnothing
, the required commutation statement is immediate from the fact that
\sigma_{!}
preserves final objects, shown above.
∎

Using the constructions above, we may now provide the putative excisive approximation functor.

Construction 2.3.7

(Excisive approximation functors)
.

Suppose
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
has final objects and
\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
has sequential colimits. We define the
\sigma
–excisive approximation functor

{P}_{\sigma}\colon\underline{\func}(\underline{{\mathcal{C}}},\underline{{\mathcal{D}}})\rightarrow\underline{\func}(\underline{{\mathcal{C}}},\underline{{\mathcal{D}}})
as

{P}_{\sigma}\coloneqq\mathop{colim}\displaylimits\big(\mathrm{id}\xlongrightarrow{\theta}{T}_{\sigma}\xlongrightarrow{\theta\circ{T}_{\sigma}}{T}_{\sigma}\circ{T}_{\sigma}\xlongrightarrow{\theta\circ{T}_{\sigma}\circ{T}_{\sigma}}\cdots\big).

Observation 2.3.8
.

Let
F\colon\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}\rightarrow\underline{\mathcal{E}}
and
G\colon\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
be functors such that
G
preserves
\sigma
–left Kan extensions and final objects. Then the canonical maps

{T}_{\sigma}(F\circ G)\rightarrow({T}_{\sigma}F)\circ G\quad\quad\quad{P}_{\sigma}(F\circ G)\rightarrow({P}_{\sigma}F)\circ G

are equivalences. The second equivalence follows immediately from the first equivalence. To see that
{T}_{\sigma}(F\circ G)\rightarrow({T}_{\sigma}F)\circ G
is an equivalence, note that
{T}_{\sigma}(F\circ G)(-)\simeq\widehat{\sigma}^{*}\sigma^{*}\mathring{\sigma}_{*}\mathring{\sigma}^{*}(F\circ G)({C}_{\sigma}(-))\simeq\widehat{\sigma}^{*}\sigma^{*}\mathring{\sigma}_{*}\mathring{\sigma}^{*}(F{C}_{\sigma}G(-))\simeq({T}_{\sigma}F)\circ G
where the second equivalence comes from the hypothesis on
G
.

As in ordinary functor calculus, the key nontrivial step in proving Goodwillie’s formula for excisive approximations is a technical lemma of Goodwillie’s, which was later streamlined by Rezk. The goal is to say that after passing to the sequential colimit as in
Construction

2.3.7
, certain poset–indexed diagrams get sent to cartesian diagrams. The idea is now to use
2.1.17
and
2.1.19
to sandwich in between two successive terms in the sequential colimit with cartesian diagrams. For more intuition, the reader should compare the following lemma with
[
20
, Lem. 6.1.1.26]
, of which our proof is a direct generalisation.

Lemma 2.3.9

(Goodwillie, Rezk)
.

Let
\sigma
be an excisable poset. Let
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}},\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{\mathcal{T}}
have final objects such that
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
is
\sigma
–left extensible and and
\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
is
\mathring{\sigma}
–right extensible. Let
F\colon\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
be a functor. Suppose
D\colon\underline{{L}}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
is
\sigma
–cocartesian. Then the canonical map
\theta_{F}:FD\rightarrow({T}_{\sigma}F)(D)
constructed above factors as
\theta_{F}\colon FD\rightarrow E\rightarrow({T}_{\sigma}F)(D)
where
E\in\underline{\mathrm{Fun}}^{\mathrm{cart}}(\underline{{L}},\underline{{\mathcal{D}}})
.

Proof.

We first make three observations:

(a)

By construction, the functor
{C}_{\sigma}\colon\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}^{\underline{{L}}}
satisfies that the adjunction unit
\sigma^{*}{C}_{\sigma}\rightarrow\widehat{\sigma}_{*}\widehat{\sigma}^{*}\sigma^{*}{C}_{\sigma}
of functors
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}^{\breve{\underline{{L}}}}
is an equivalence.

(b)

Let
x\in\underline{{L}}
. By
Proposition

2.1.17
, the diagram
(x\vee-)^{*}D\colon\underline{{L}}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
is
\sigma
–cocartesian.

(c)

Consider the composition

\underline{\func}(\underline{{L}},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})\xlongrightarrow{F_{*}}\underline{\func}(\underline{{L}},\underline{{\mathcal{D}}})\xlongrightarrow{\vee^{*}}\underline{\func}(\underline{{L}}\times\underline{{L}},\underline{{\mathcal{D}}})\xlongrightarrow{(\mathrm{id}\times\mathring{\sigma})^{*}}\underline{\func}(\underline{{L}}\times\mathring{\underline{{L}}},\underline{{\mathcal{D}}})

(5)

We claim that the composition
Eq.

5
factors through
\underline{\mathrm{Fun}}^{\mathrm{cart}}(\underline{{L}},\underline{\func}(\mathring{\underline{{L}}},\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}))
. This may be checked pointwise by fixing an arbitrary
x\in\mathring{\underline{{L}}}
. So let
Y\in\underline{\func}(\underline{{L}},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})
. In this case, we get that
((\mathrm{id}\times\mathring{\sigma})^{*}F\vee^{*}Y)(x)\colon\underline{{L}}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
is given by the functor
FY\circ(-\vee x)
, which is indeed cartesian by
Proposition

2.1.19
as claimed. Observe that, in particular,
(\mathrm{id}\times\mathring{\sigma})_{*}(\mathrm{id}\times\mathring{\sigma})^{*}F\vee^{*}Y\in\underline{\func}(\underline{{L}},\underline{\func}(\underline{{L}},\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}))
is in fact an object in
\underline{\mathrm{Fun}}^{\mathrm{cart}}(\underline{{L}},\underline{\func}(\underline{{L}},\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}))
, and so evaluating at
\varnothing
in the second
\underline{{L}}
variable gives that
(\mathrm{id}\times\sigma\widehat{\sigma})^{*}(\mathrm{id}\times\mathring{\sigma})_{*}(\mathrm{id}\times\mathring{\sigma})^{*}F\vee^{*}Y\in\underline{\mathrm{Fun}}^{\mathrm{cart}}(\underline{{L}},\underline{{\mathcal{D}}})
.

Now, note that for each
x\in{\underline{{L}}}
, we have an identity map
\widehat{\sigma}^{*}\sigma^{*}(x\vee-)^{*}D\longrightarrow\widehat{\sigma}^{*}\sigma^{*}{C}_{\sigma}(D(x))
on the object
D(x)\in\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
. Thus, adjointing
\widehat{\sigma}^{*}
over, we obtain a map
\sigma^{*}(x\vee-)^{*}D\longrightarrow\widehat{\sigma}_{*}\widehat{\sigma}^{*}\sigma^{*}{C}_{\sigma}(D(x))\simeq\sigma^{*}{C}_{\sigma}(D(x))
, where the equivalence is by point (a). By adjointing now
\sigma^{*}
over, we obtain a map
(x\vee-)^{*}D\simeq\sigma_{!}\sigma^{*}(x\vee-)^{*}D\longrightarrow{C}_{\sigma}(D(x))
in
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}^{\underline{{L}}}
, where the equivalence is by point (b). Letting
x\in{\underline{{L}}}
vary, we obtain a morphism in
\underline{\func}({\underline{{L}}},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}^{\underline{{L}}})

(\bullet\vee-)^{*}D\longrightarrow{C}_{\sigma}(D(\bullet))

where the
\bullet
variable corresponds to the first
{\underline{{L}}}
variable. Applying
(\mathrm{id}\times\mathring{\sigma})_{*}(\mathrm{id}\times\mathring{\sigma})^{*}F
to this morphism and precomposing with the adjunction unit gives

F(\bullet\vee-)^{*}D\longrightarrow(\mathrm{id}\times\mathring{\sigma})_{*}(\mathrm{id}\times\mathring{\sigma})^{*}F\big((\bullet\vee-)^{*}D\big)\longrightarrow(\mathrm{id}\times\mathring{\sigma})_{*}(\mathrm{id}\times\mathring{\sigma})^{*}F{C}_{\sigma}(D(\bullet))

By evaluating at
\varnothing
in the second
\underline{{L}}
variable (i.e. by applying
(\mathrm{id}\times\sigma\widehat{\sigma})^{*}
to this map) and defining
E(\bullet)\coloneqq(\mathrm{id}\times\sigma\widehat{\sigma})^{*}(\mathrm{id}\times\mathring{\sigma})_{*}(\mathrm{id}\times\mathring{\sigma})^{*}F\big((\bullet\vee-)^{*}D\big)
, we obtain the morphism

FD(\bullet)\longrightarrow E(\bullet)\longrightarrow({T}_{\sigma}F)(D)(\bullet)

in
\underline{\func}(\underline{{L}},\underline{{\mathcal{D}}})
, which is equivalent to
\theta_{F}
from
Eq.

4
by
Lemma

2.3.6
since
\theta_{F}
was constructed using the
\mathring{\sigma}^{*}\dashv\mathring{\sigma}_{*}
adjunction unit, which in our present two variable setting, corresponds to the adjunction unit
(\mathrm{id}\times\mathring{\sigma})^{*}\dashv(\mathrm{id}\times\mathring{\sigma})_{*}
. Furthermore, by point (c) above, we know that
E\in\underline{\mathrm{Fun}}^{\mathrm{cart}}(\underline{{L}},\underline{{\mathcal{D}}})
. This completes the proof.
∎

By passing to a sequential colimit of cartesian diagrams, we thus see that applying
P_{\sigma}
yields a
\sigma
–excisive functor.

Lemma 2.3.10

(Goodwillie approximations are excisive, “
[
20
, Lem. 6.1.1.33]
”)
.

Let
\sigma
be an excisable poset. Let
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}},\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{\mathcal{T}}
have final objects such that
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
is
\sigma
–left extensible and and
\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
is
\mathring{\sigma}
–right extensible. Let
F\colon\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
be a functor. Then
{P}_{\sigma}F\colon\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
is
\sigma
–excisive.

Proof.

Let
D\colon\underline{{L}}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
be
\sigma
–cocartesian. We want to show that
({P}_{\sigma}F)(D)
is cartesian. By definition, we have
({P}_{\sigma}F)(D)\coloneqq\mathop{colim}\displaylimits\big(FD\rightarrow({T}_{\sigma}F)(D)\rightarrow({T}_{\sigma}^{2}F)(D)\rightarrow\cdots\big)
.
By the Rezk
Lemma

2.3.9
, we have a cartesian factorisation

({T}_{\sigma}^{k}F)(D)\rightarrow E_{k}\rightarrow({T}_{\sigma}^{k+1}F)(D)

so that we could alternatively have computed
({P}_{\sigma}F)(D)
as a sequential colimit of
E_{0}\rightarrow E_{1}\rightarrow E_{2}\rightarrow\cdots
.
Since each
E_{i}
is cartesian in
\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
, we thus obtain by
\sigma
–differentiability of
\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
that the sequential colimit
({P}_{\sigma}F)(D)
is cartesian, as required.
∎

Lemma 2.3.11

(Idempotence of Goodwillie approximations, “
[
20
, Lem. 6.1.1.35]
”)
.

Let
\sigma
be an excisable poset. Let
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}},\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{\mathcal{T}}
have final objects such that
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
is
\sigma
–left extensible and
\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
is
\sigma
–differentiable. Let
F\colon\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
be a functor. Then
{P}_{\sigma}\theta\colon{P}_{\sigma}F\rightarrow{P}_{\sigma}{T}_{\sigma}F
is an equivalence. Consequently, the canonical transformation
{P}_{\sigma}F\rightarrow{P}_{\sigma}{P}_{\sigma}F
is an equivalence.

Proof.

By
\sigma
–differentiability of
\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
, the functor
{P}_{\sigma}\colon\underline{\func}(\underline{{\mathcal{C}}},\underline{{\mathcal{D}}})\rightarrow\underline{\func}(\underline{{\mathcal{C}}},\underline{{\mathcal{D}}})
commutes with
\sigma
–right Kan extensions, and so the canonical map

{P}_{\sigma}{T}_{\sigma}F\longrightarrow\widehat{\sigma}^{*}\sigma^{*}\mathring{\sigma}_{*}\mathring{\sigma}^{*}\big({P}_{\sigma}(F\circ{C}_{\sigma})\big)\xrightarrow{\simeq}\widehat{\sigma}^{*}\sigma^{*}\mathring{\sigma}_{*}\mathring{\sigma}^{*}\big(({P}_{\sigma}F)\circ{C}_{\sigma}\big)={T}_{\sigma}{P}_{\sigma}F

is an equivalence, where the second equivalence is by
Lemma

2.3.6
and
Observation

2.3.8
. But then
{P}_{\sigma}F
was
\sigma
–excisive by
Lemma

2.3.10
, and so since
{C}_{\sigma}\in\underline{\func}(\underline{{\mathcal{C}}},\underline{\mathrm{Fun}}^{{\sigma}_{!}}(\underline{{L}},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}))
, we obtain that the canonical map

{P}_{\sigma}F\rightarrow\widehat{\sigma}^{*}\sigma^{*}\mathring{\sigma}_{*}\mathring{\sigma}^{*}\big(({P}_{\sigma}F)\circ{C}_{\sigma}\big)
is an equivalence. Therefore, by the commuting triangle
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P}_{\sigma}F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P}_{\sigma}{T}_{\sigma}F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\sigma}^{*}\sigma^{*}\mathring{\sigma}_{*}\mathring{\sigma}^{*}\big(({P}_{\sigma}F)\circ{C}_{\sigma}\big)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P}_{\sigma}\theta_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{can}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{can}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

we get that
{P}_{\sigma}\theta_{F}
is an equivalence, as required.
∎

We are at last ready to state and prove
Theorem

A
.

Theorem 2.3.12

(Excisive Bousfield localisation)
.

Let
\sigma\colon\breve{\underline{{L}}}\subseteq\underline{{L}}
be an excisable poset. Let
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
have a final object and
\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
is
\sigma
–differentiable. Then the fully faithful inclusion
\underline{\mathrm{Exc}}^{\sigma}(\underline{{\mathcal{C}}},\underline{{\mathcal{D}}})\subseteq\underline{\func}(\underline{{\mathcal{C}}},\underline{{\mathcal{D}}})
admits a left adjoint given by
{P}_{\sigma}
. Furthermore, if
\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
has finite parametrised limits and they commute with sequential colimits, then
{P}_{\sigma}
is even parametrised left exact.

Proof.

By
Lemma

2.3.10
, the endofunctor
{P}_{\sigma}
on
\underline{\func}(\underline{{\mathcal{C}}},\underline{{\mathcal{D}}})
factors through
\underline{\mathrm{Exc}}^{\sigma}(\underline{{\mathcal{C}}},\underline{{\mathcal{D}}})
. On the other hand, if
F\in\underline{\func}(\underline{{\mathcal{C}}},\underline{{\mathcal{D}}})
were
\sigma
–excisive, then since
{C}_{\sigma}\in\underline{\func}(\underline{{\mathcal{C}}},\underline{\mathrm{Fun}}^{{\sigma}_{!}}(\underline{{L}},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}))
, the map
F\rightarrow{T}_{\sigma}F
is an equivalence (which in particular implies that
{P}_{\sigma}F
is also
\sigma
–excisive). Iterating this observation, we get that
F\rightarrow{P}_{\sigma}F
is an equivalence.

To see that it is a Bousfield localisation we just need to show that the two maps

{P}_{\sigma}\theta_{F},\>\theta_{{P}_{\sigma}F}\colon{P}_{\sigma}F\longrightarrow{P}_{\sigma}{P}_{\sigma}F

are equivalences. The case of
{P}_{\sigma}\theta_{F}
is covered already by
Lemma

2.3.11
, whereas that of
\theta_{{P}_{\sigma}F}
is also done since
{P}_{\sigma}F
was
\sigma
–excisive. This completes the proof of the first part. The second statement about parametrised left exactness of
{P}_{\sigma}
under the extra hypothesis on
\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
is immediate from the explicit construction of
{P}_{\sigma}
given in
Construction

2.3.7
.
∎

The next result is a generalisation of the Goodwillie calculus fact that
(n-1)
–excisive functors are automatically also
n
–excisive, and for it, recall the induced excisable poset structure
\sigma_{a}
associated to an object
a\in\underline{{L}}
in an excisable poset
\sigma\colon\breve{\underline{{L}}}\subseteq\underline{{L}}
.

Proposition 2.3.13
.

Let
\sigma
be an excisable poset,
a\in\underline{{L}}
an object, and
F\colon\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
a
\sigma_{a}
–excisive functor. Then
F
is also
\sigma
–excisive.

Proof.

Let
X\colon\underline{{L}}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
be a
\sigma
–cocartesian diagram. By
Proposition

2.2.5
, we know that all
a
–faces of
X
are
\sigma_{a}
–cocartesian. Hence, by hypothesis, every
a
–face of
FX\colon\underline{{L}}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
is
\sigma_{a}
–cartesian. By
Proposition

2.2.7
, this implies that
FX
is cartesian, as required.
∎

Remark 2.3.14
.

As a consequence of
Theorem

2.3.12
and
Proposition

2.3.13
, we obtain a natural transformation
{P}_{\sigma}\rightarrow{P}_{\sigma_{a}}
for every object
a\in\underline{{L}}
. In particular, if we have a countable number of excisable posets equipped with an object
(\sigma_{i}\colon\breve{\underline{{L}}}_{i}\subseteq\underline{{L}}_{i},a_{i}\in\underline{{L}}_{i})
such that
\sigma_{i}\cong\sigma_{i+1,a_{i+1}}
, then will obtain a Goodwillie tower

\mathrm{id}\longrightarrow\cdots\rightarrow{P}_{\sigma_{2}}\rightarrow{P}_{\sigma_{1}}\rightarrow{P}_{\sigma_{0}}

associated to this sequence of excisable posets.

3
Parametrised cubical excision

Our goal is to employ the abstract theory of
Section

2
to prove
Theorem

B
and
Theorem

C
by specialising to the case of atomic orbital base categories. We first recall the cubical constructions from
[
16
,
\lx@sectionsign 3
]
in
Section

3.1
and further develop some basic properties, a crucial one being
Proposition

3.1.14
that external indexed products of singleton cocartesian cubes are again singleton cocartesian; using these cubes, we construct the parametrised spheres that will generalise the regular representation spheres from the equivariant context in
Section

3.2
; we then justify in
Section

3.3
that we may port the theory of
Section

2
to the present context of cubes before proceeding to prove
Theorem

B
and
Theorem

C
in
Section

3.4
. As before, recall the notations and conventions from
Section

1.1
.

3.1
Cubes

Definition 3.1.1

(
[
25
, Def. 4.1]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a small category. We say that it is
atomic
if whenever we have
f:W\rightarrow V
and
g:V\rightarrow W
in
{\mathcal{T}}
such that
g\circ f
is an equivalence, then
f
and
g
were already inverse equivalences; and we say that it is
orbital
if the finite coproduct cocompletion
\mathrm{Fin}_{\mathcal{T}}
admits finite pullbacks.

Throughout this section,
{\mathcal{T}}
will always be an atomic orbital category unless otherwise specified. For examples of such categories, we refer the reader to
Example

3.4.11
. We first repeat some cubical constructions and facts established in
[
16
, §3]
for the reader’s convenience.

Notation 3.1.2
.

We write
\underline{\Delta}^{1}\in\underline{\mathrm{Cat}}
for the constant parametrised category with value
\Delta^{1}
, and we will often be vague about the base category we are parametrising over since we will often need to change base categories when we consider
\underline{\Delta}^{1}
.

Definition 3.1.3
.

Let
w\colon W\rightarrow V
be a morphism in
\in{\mathrm{Fin}}_{\mathcal{T}}
and let
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
be a
\mathcal{T}
–category. By
the parametrised
w
–cube
we will mean the
\mathcal{T}_{/V}
–category
w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}
and by
a parametrised
w
–cube in
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
we will mean a
\mathcal{T}_{/V}
–functor
Q\colon w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\rightarrow V^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
.

Observation 3.1.4
.

An important feature of the parametrised cubes
w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}
is that the only global points they have are the initial and final objects
\varnothing
and
\mathbbm{1}
since a map
\underline{\ast}\rightarrow w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}
corresponds by adjunction to a map
\underline{\ast}\rightarrow\underline{\Delta}^{1}
. The other parametrised points of
w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}
will thus come from
B
–points
b_{!}\underline{\ast}\rightarrow w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}
where
b\colon B\rightarrow V
is the unique map in
{\mathcal{T}}_{/V}
(where
b\in{\mathcal{T}}_{/V}
is not the final object).

Fact 3.1.5

(
[
16
, Prop. 3.1.2]
)
.

The parametrised cubes
w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}
are all parametrised posets, i.e. they belong to the full subcategory
\func(\mathcal{T}_{/V}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Poset})\subseteq\func(\mathcal{T}_{/V}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Cat})
. In fact, these are fibrewise given by cubes of various dimensions.

Construction 3.1.6

(Points in cubes are subsets)
.

Suppose
{\mathcal{T}}
has a final object
V
and
w\colon W\rightarrow V
is the unique map to the final object. Let
B\in{\mathcal{T}}
and
b\colon B\rightarrow V
the unique map. We would like to describe a
B
–point
y\colon b_{!}\underline{\ast}\rightarrow w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}
. First of all, write
b^{*}W=U_{1}\amalg\cdots\amalg U_{n}
for the decomposition of
b^{*}W
into orbits over
B
and
u_{i}\colon U_{i}\rightarrow B
for the structure maps. Adjoining over
y
we get a map

\underline{\ast}\longrightarrow b^{*}w_{*}w^{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\simeq u_{1*}u_{1}^{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\times\cdots\times u_{k*}u_{k}^{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}

Adjoining over each of the component yields maps

u_{1}^{*}\underline{\ast}\longrightarrow u_{1}^{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}

which is either the constant functor at 0 or 1. Hence the
B
–point
y
can be viewed as a choice of orbits in the decomposition
b^{*}W=U_{1}\amalg\cdots\amalg U_{n}
. In general, given any map
w\colon W\to V
in
{\mathcal{T}}
where
V
is not necessarily final, we may apply the construction above in
{\mathcal{T}}_{/V}
.

The following is the key construction in the theory of parametrised cubes.

Construction 3.1.7

(Singleton inclusion,
[
16
, Cons. 3.1.5]
)
.

Let
w\colon W\rightarrow V
be a map in
\underline{\mathrm{Fin}}_{\mathcal{T}}
. We would like to construct a map

\varphi_{w}\colon(w_{!}\underline{\ast})^{\underline{\triangleleft}}\longrightarrow w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}

(6)

which generalises the inclusion of the subsets of size at most 1 in Goodwillie’s definition of strong cocartesianness. For this, we first construct a map

\psi_{w}\colon w_{!}\underline{\ast}\longrightarrow w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}.

First note by atomic orbitality that we have the pullback in
\mathrm{Fin}_{\mathcal{T}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W\coprod C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\coprod c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\coprod\overline{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}

(7)

where
C
is some object in
\mathrm{Fin}_{\mathcal{T}}
. In particular, we have the decomposition

w^{*}w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\simeq\mathrm{id}_{*}\mathrm{id}^{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\times\overline{c}_{*}c^{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\simeq\underline{\Delta}^{1}\times\overline{c}_{*}c^{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}

Now by adjunction, to construct
\psi_{w}
it suffices to construct its adjoint
\overline{\psi}_{w}\colon\underline{\ast}\rightarrow w^{*}w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}
. By the decomposition above, this is equivalent to constructing maps

\underline{\ast}\rightarrow\underline{\Delta}^{1}\quad\text{and}\quad\big(\underline{\ast}\rightarrow\overline{{c}}_{*}c^{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\big)\Leftrightarrow\big(\overline{c}^{*}\underline{\ast}\simeq c^{*}\underline{\ast}\rightarrow c^{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\big)

To this end, we declare the first map to be the inclusion of the target
\{1\}\rightarrow\Delta^{1}
and the second map to be
c^{*}
applied to the inclusion of the source
\{0\}\rightarrow\Delta^{1}
. This yields the map
\psi_{w}
, which one should think of the inclusion of the singletons in a cube. By
[
16
, Cons. 3.1.3]
, the map
\psi_{w}
constructed above induces the desired map
Eq.

6
.

Remark 3.1.8
.

The maps
\varphi_{w}
generalise the inclusion of those subsets of size at most 1 into a cube. Namely if
V
is a final object of
{\mathcal{T}}
and
W
is equivalent to a finite coproduct
\coprod_{n}V
living over
V
via the fold map, then
w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}
is the constant
{\mathcal{T}}
-category on the category
\prod_{n}\Delta^{1}
and the map
\varphi_{w}
is precisely given levelwise by the standard inclusion of subsets of size at most 1. See
[
16
, Ex. 3.1.6]
for more details.

Fact 3.1.9
.

Here are some basic properties of the construction above.

(1)

The singleton inclusion map is stable under basechange
[
16
, Rmk. 3.1.7]
in that for
b\colon B\rightarrow V
a map in
{\mathcal{T}}
, writing
\overline{w}\colon b^{*}W\rightarrow B
for the basechange of
w\colon W\rightarrow V
along
b
, we get
b^{*}\varphi_{w}\simeq\varphi_{\overline{w}}
.

(2)

The map
\varphi_{w}\colon(w_{!}\underline{\ast})^{\underline{\triangleleft}}\rightarrow w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}
is fully faithful by
[
16
, Cor. 3.1.8]
.

Terminology 3.1.10
.

It will be convenient to use also the phrase
singleton cocartesian
to denote any parametrised cube which is left Kan extended from the singletons, i.e. those which are
\varphi_{w}
–cocartesian in the sense of
Definition

2.1.16
. This allows us to be vague about which
w
we are using. Analogously, we will use the term
singleton cartesian
for the obvious dual notion. More precisely, a cubical diagram is singleton cartesian if it is right Kan extended along

(w_{!}\ast)^{\underline{\triangleright}}\simeq((w_{!}\ast)^{\underline{\triangleleft}})^{\mathrm{op}}\xhookrightarrow{\varphi_{w}^{\mathrm{op}}}(w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1})^{\mathrm{op}}\simeq w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}.

Having set up the basic language, our next task is to further develop the theory by proving that the external indexed product of any singleton cocartesian cube is again singleton cocartesian in
Proposition

3.1.14
. This seems to us an under–appreciated fact in ordinary Goodwillie calculus since the inductive proof is relatively painless there. We have no recourse to such inductive proofs in this setting, and so the proof we provide will be necessarily categorical and “coordinate–free”.

To this end, we first formalise the process of taking the “external indexed product” of diagrams and record a basic property of this construction.

Construction 3.1.11

(External indexed products of diagrams)
.

Let
a\colon V\rightarrow A
be a map in
{\mathcal{T}}
. Let
\underline{I},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{{\mathcal{T}}_{/V}}
. We now construct a natural map

\varepsilon^{*}\colon a_{*}\underline{\func}(\underline{I},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})\longrightarrow\underline{\func}(a_{*}\underline{I},a_{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})

which, informally, takes a diagram
X\colon\underline{I}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
and produces the new diagram
a_{*}X=\varepsilon^{*}X\colon a_{*}\underline{I}\rightarrow a_{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
. This is achieved as follows: let
\varepsilon\colon a^{*}a_{*}\underline{I}\rightarrow\underline{I}
be the adjunction counit. We then obtain a map

\underline{\func}(\underline{I},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})\xlongrightarrow{\varepsilon^{*}}\underline{\func}(a^{*}a_{*}\underline{I},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})

Applying
a_{*}
to this map and noting that
a_{*}\underline{\func}(a^{*}a_{*}\underline{I},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})\simeq\underline{\func}(a_{*}\underline{I},a_{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})
, we obtain the desired map.

Now suppose that
\underline{I}
has an initial object. Observe that by atomicity of
{\mathcal{T}}
, the adjunction counit
\varepsilon\colon a^{*}a_{*}\underline{I}\rightarrow\underline{I}
is given by projecting onto the
\underline{I}
component in the decomposition
a^{*}a_{*}\underline{I}\simeq\underline{I}\times c_{*}c^{*}\underline{I}
associated to the decomposition
V\times_{A}V\simeq V\amalg C
. Since
c_{*}c^{*}\underline{I}
has an initial object, we have an adjunction
\varnothing\colon\underline{\ast}\rightleftharpoons c_{*}c^{*}\underline{I}\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu\pi
where
\pi
is the unique functor. Taking the product with
\underline{I}
then yields the adjunction

\varnothing\colon\underline{I}\rightleftharpoons a^{*}a_{*}\underline{I}\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu\varepsilon

Proposition 3.1.12
.

Let
j\colon\underline{I}\rightarrow\underline{J}
be a functor which preserves the initial object. Then the square
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{*}\underline{\func}(\underline{I},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\func}(a_{*}\underline{I},a_{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{*}\underline{\func}(\underline{J},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\func}(a_{*}\underline{J},a_{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(a_{*}j)_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon^{*}}

commutes.

Proof.

From the adjunction
\varnothing\dashv\varepsilon
of
Construction

3.1.11
together with the fact that indexed products preserve adjunctions
[
15
, Lem. 4.3.2]
, we obtain an adjunction
\varepsilon^{*}\colon a_{*}\underline{\func}(\underline{I},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})\rightleftharpoons\underline{\func}(a_{*}\underline{I},a_{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu\varnothing^{*}
. Now passing everything in sight to their right adjoints in the square of the proposition yields the square
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{*}\underline{\func}(\underline{I},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\func}(a_{*}\underline{I},a_{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{*}\underline{\func}(\underline{J},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\func}(a_{*}\underline{J},a_{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varnothing^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(a_{*}j)^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varnothing^{*}}

which clearly commutes since
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{*}a_{*}\underline{I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{J}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{*}a_{*}\underline{J}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varnothing}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{*}a_{*}j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varnothing}

commutes by our assumption that
j
preserves the initial object. Hence the left adjoint square commutes too, as required.
∎

Construction 3.1.13

(Pushforward of singletons)
.

Let
w\colon W\rightarrow V
be in
\mathrm{Fin}_{\mathcal{T}}
and
a\colon V\rightarrow A
be in
{\mathcal{T}}
. Recall the map
\varnothing\colon(w_{!}\underline{\ast})^{\underline{\triangleleft}}\rightarrow a^{*}a_{*}(w_{!}\underline{\ast})^{\underline{\triangleleft}}
given in
Construction

3.1.11
. We would like to construct a factorisation of it that looks like
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{*}(a_{!}w_{!}\underline{\ast})^{\underline{\triangleleft}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(w_{!}\underline{\ast})^{\underline{\triangleleft}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{*}a_{*}(w_{!}\underline{\ast})^{\underline{\triangleleft}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{*}\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varnothing}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}

By construction all functors in the triangle above will preserve initial objects and so it suffices to construct the factorisation
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{*}a_{!}w_{!}\underline{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{!}\underline{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{*}a_{*}(w_{!}\underline{\ast})^{\underline{\triangleleft}},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{*}\overline{\theta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varnothing}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{z}}

which we may then extend uniquely to a triangle of the first kind. We simply set
\overline{z}
to be the adjunction unit, then by adjunction there is a unique
\overline{\theta}
making the triangle commute. We use this choice, thus completing the construction.

Crucially, the map
\theta
interacts well with the maps

\varphi_{w}\colon(w_{!}\underline{\ast})^{\underline{\triangleleft}}\rightarrow w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}

of
Construction

3.1.7
, by which we mean that the composite

(a_{!}w_{!}\underline{\ast})^{\underline{\triangleleft}}\xlongrightarrow{\theta}a_{*}(w_{!}\underline{\ast})^{\underline{\triangleleft}}\xlongrightarrow{a_{*}\varphi_{w}}a_{*}w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}

is equivalent to
\varphi_{aw}
. To prove this, we observe that since all three functors preserve the initial object, it suffices to show that the composite

a_{!}w_{!}\underline{\ast}\xlongrightarrow{\overline{\theta}}a_{*}(w_{!}\underline{\ast})^{\underline{\triangleleft}}\xlongrightarrow{a_{*}\varphi_{w}}a_{*}w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}

is equivalent to
\psi_{aw}
, the singleton inclusion map. Using the orbital decomposition
V\times_{A}V\simeq V\amalg C
and adjoining the
a_{!}
over, this composite yields the composite
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{!}\underline{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{*}a_{*}(w_{!}\underline{\ast})^{\underline{\triangleleft}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{*}a_{*}w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{!}\underline{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(w_{!}\underline{\ast})^{\underline{\triangleleft}}\times c_{*}c^{*}(w_{!}\underline{\ast})^{\underline{\triangleleft}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\times c_{*}c^{*}w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varnothing}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{*}a_{*}\varphi_{w}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{incl}\times\varnothing}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{w}\times c_{*}c^{*}\varphi_{w}}

Hence, the datum of this composite is equivalent to the data of the maps

w_{!}\underline{\ast}\xlongrightarrow{\psi_{w}}w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\quad\quad\quad\quad w_{!}\underline{\ast}\xlongrightarrow{\varnothing}c_{*}c^{*}w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}

This is precisely the data of maps defining the map
\psi_{aw}\colon a_{!}w_{!}\underline{\ast}\rightarrow a_{*}w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}
.

We are now ready to show that indexed products preserve singleton cocartesian cubes.

Proposition 3.1.14
.

Suppose
X\colon w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
is a singleton cocartesian
w
–cube and let
a\colon V\rightarrow A
be in
{\mathcal{T}}
. In this case,
a_{*}X\colon a_{*}w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\rightarrow a_{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
is then a singleton cocartesian
a\circ w
–cube.

Proof.

By the triangle in
Construction

3.1.13
, we have a commuting triangle
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\func}(a^{*}(a_{!}w_{!}\underline{\ast})^{\underline{\triangleleft}},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\func}((w_{!}\underline{\ast})^{\underline{\triangleleft}},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\func}(a^{*}a_{*}(w_{!}\underline{\ast})^{\underline{\triangleleft}},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(a^{*}\theta)^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varnothing^{*}}

Passing to left adjoints and using the
\varnothing\dashv\varepsilon
adjunction, we obtain the commuting triangle
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\func}(a^{*}(a_{!}w_{!}\underline{\ast})^{\underline{\triangleleft}},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\func}((w_{!}\underline{\ast})^{\underline{\triangleleft}},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\func}(a^{*}a_{*}(w_{!}\underline{\ast})^{\underline{\triangleleft}},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(a^{*}\theta)_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon^{*}}

Applying
a_{*}
to this triangle and combining the result with the square from
Proposition

3.1.12
, we obtain the following big commuting diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\func}((a_{!}w_{!}\underline{\ast})^{\underline{\triangleleft}},a_{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{*}\underline{\func}((w_{!}\underline{\ast})^{\underline{\triangleleft}},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\func}(a_{*}(w_{!}\underline{\ast})^{\underline{\triangleleft}},a_{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{*}\underline{\func}(w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\func}(a_{*}w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1},a_{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\varphi_{aw})_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\varphi_{w})_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(a_{*}\varphi_{w})_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon^{*}}

where the equivalence of the right vertical composition with the bent arrow is by the second paragraph from
Construction

3.1.13
.

Now
X
being a singleton cocartesian
w
–cube means that there is a
Y\colon(w_{!}\underline{\ast})^{\underline{\triangleleft}}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
such that
X\simeq(\varphi_{w})_{!}Y
. Therefore, by the big diagram, we see that

a_{*}X=\varepsilon^{*}X\simeq(\varphi_{aw})_{!}z_{!}Y\colon a_{*}w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\longrightarrow a_{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}

as desired.
∎

3.2
Suspensions and spheres

In this subsection, we use the parametrised cubes to construct parametrised suspension and loop functors as well as the parametrised spheres that will feature in
Theorem

B
and
Theorem

C
.

For the reader’s convenience, we record the following result of Shah about general base categories
{\mathcal{T}}
.

Proposition 3.2.1

(
[
29
, Prop. 5.5]
)
.

Let
\underline{I}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{{\mathcal{T}}}
,
p\colon\underline{I}\rightarrow\underline{\ast}
the unique map, and
u\in{\mathcal{T}}
. Let
\varphi\in\func_{{\mathcal{T}}}(\underline{I},\underline{\mathcal{S}}_{*})
. Then
(p_{!}\varphi)(u)\simeq\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{I_{u}}(I_{u}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{*})
. This holds similarly when we replace
\underline{\mathcal{S}}_{*}
with
\underline{\mathrm{Cat}}
.

Construction 3.2.2

(Parametrised suspensions, loops, and spheres)
.

Let
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
be a parametrised cocomplete category with a final object. Let
w\colon W\rightarrow V
be a map in
\mathrm{Fin}_{{\mathcal{T}}}
and write
\widehat{\sigma}_{w}\colon\underline{\ast}\hookrightarrow w_{*}\Delta^{1}
for the inclusion of the initial object. Then we define the
parametrised suspension
endofunctor
\Sigma^{w}
on
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
as the composite of
{\mathcal{T}}_{/V}
–functors

\Sigma^{w}\colon V^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\xhookrightarrow{\widehat{\sigma}_{w*}}\underline{\func}(u_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\backslash\mathbbm{1},V^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})\xlongrightarrow{\mathop{colim}\displaylimits}V^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}.

If, additionally,
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
is pointed and parametrised complete, then we also define the
parametrised loop
endofunctor as the composite of
{\mathcal{T}}_{/V}
–functors

\Omega^{w}\colon V^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\xhookrightarrow{(\widehat{\sigma}_{w}^{\mathrm{op}})_{!}}\underline{\func}(w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\backslash\varnothing,V^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})\xlongrightarrow{\lim}V^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}.

In the special case when
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}=\underline{\mathcal{S}}_{*}
, we define the
w
–parametrised sphere
to be

S^{w}\coloneqq\Sigma^{w}S^{0}\in\underline{\mathcal{S}}_{*}.

This terminology is justified since
S^{w}\in\func(({\mathcal{T}}_{/V})^{\mathrm{op}},\mathcal{S}_{*})
is given fibrewise by spheres of various dimensions. This is because
w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\backslash\mathbbm{1}
is fibrewise given by punctured cubes by
Fact

3.1.5
, and so applying
Proposition

3.2.1
, we see that
S^{w}
is fibrewise given by some suspension of
S^{0}
.

As one may expect, the suspension and loop functors participate in an adjunction.

Proposition 3.2.3
.

Let
{\mathcal{T}}
be atomic orbital. For every morphism
w\colon W\rightarrow V
in
\mathrm{Fin}_{{\mathcal{T}}}
, and for
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
a pointed parametrised bicomplete category, there is a suspension–loop adjunction
\Sigma^{w}\colon V^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\rightleftharpoons V^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu\Omega^{w}
.

Proof.

Let
X,Y\in V^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
. Write
\sigma_{X}\colon w_{*}\Delta^{1}\backslash\mathbbm{1}\rightarrow V^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
and
\lambda_{Y}\colon w_{*}\Delta^{1}\backslash 0\rightarrow V^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
for the functors defining
\Sigma^{w}X
and
\Omega^{w}Y
respectively. Since
\mathchoice{\mbox{\uline{\phantom{$\displaystyle\map$}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.5pt}[0.0pt]{$\displaystyle\map$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.19133pt\raisebox{0.46193pt}[0.0pt]{$\displaystyle\map$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.35355pt\raisebox{0.35355pt}[0.0pt]{$\displaystyle\map$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.46193pt\raisebox{0.19133pt}[0.0pt]{$\displaystyle\map$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{$\displaystyle\map$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip
