which are clearly natural in
X
and
Y
.
∎

Definition 3.2.4
.

Let
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
be a pointed parametrised bicomplete category and
w\colon W\rightarrow V
a morphism in
\mathrm{Fin}_{{\mathcal{T}}}
. We say that it is:

(1)

w
–spherically faithful
if the endofunctor
\Sigma^{w}\colon V^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\rightarrow V^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
of
{\mathcal{T}}_{/V}
–categories is fully faithful and
\Omega^{w}
commutes with sequential colimits,

(2)

w
–spherically invertible
if the endofunctor
\Sigma^{w}\colon V^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\rightarrow V^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
of
{\mathcal{T}}_{/V}
–categories is an equivalence.

We say that
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
is
spherically faithful
(resp.
spherically invertible
) if
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
is
w
–spherically faithful (resp.
w
–spherically invertible) for all morphisms
w\colon W\rightarrow V
in
\mathrm{Fin}_{{\mathcal{T}}}
.

Example 3.2.5
.

Consider the case
{\mathcal{T}}=\mathcal{O}_{G}
for a finite group
G
and the map
w\colon W\rightarrow V
is the unique map
G/H\rightarrow G/G
in
\mathcal{O}_{G}
. Computing the parametrised colimit
\Sigma^{w}(-)
in topological
G
-spaces one finds that it is given by smashing with the representation sphere
S^{\widetilde{\mathbb{R}[G/H]}}
of the reduced permutation representation, see
[
7
, Ex. 1.18]
for example.

The reader may want to compare the definition above with
[
20
, Prop. 1.4.2.11]
to get a feeling for how it will become relevant for our excisiveness considerations later.

Example 3.2.6
.

Let
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
be a pointed parametrised bicomplete category equipped with a symmetric monoidal structure which preserves parametrised colimits in each variable. Suppose that the object
S^{w}\coloneqq\Sigma^{w}\mathbbm{1}\in\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
is invertible. Then
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
is
w
–spherically invertible. One can show that under our assumptions on the symmetric monoidal structure on
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
there is a natural equivalence of endofunctors
\Sigma^{w}(-)\simeq S^{w}\otimes(-)
on
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
. Thus, since
S^{w}\otimes(-)
is an automorphism, so is
\Sigma^{w}(-)
and by
Proposition

3.2.3
, the inverse is necessarily given by
\Omega^{w}(-)
.

To end this subsection, we provide a “calculus” for manipulating these spheres. Before stating the following proposition, we establish the following notation: for a map
w\colon W\rightarrow V
in
\mathrm{Fin}_{{\mathcal{T}}}
, we write
w_{+}
for the map
W\sqcup V\xrightarrow{w\sqcup\mathrm{id}}V
.

In the third part of the next result, we shall also need the notion of
{\mathcal{T}}
-distributive symmetric monoidal structures as introduced by Nardin in his thesis
[
26
]
, which was also further treated in
[
24
]
. See also
[
16
, Recoll. 2.1.31]
.

Proposition 3.2.7
.

Let
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
be a pointed
{\mathcal{T}}
–cocomplete category. Let
u\colon U\rightarrow V
and
w\colon W\rightarrow V
be maps in
\mathrm{Fin}_{{\mathcal{T}}}
. Then:

(1)

\Sigma^{w_{+}}\simeq\Sigma\Sigma^{w}\colon V^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\rightarrow V^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
,

(2)

\Sigma^{u\sqcup w}\simeq\Sigma^{u_{+}}\Sigma^{w}\colon V^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\rightarrow V^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
. In particular, we have
\Sigma^{u\sqcup w_{+}}\simeq\Sigma^{u_{+}}\Sigma^{w_{+}}\colon V^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\rightarrow V^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
,

(3)

Suppose
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
is a pointed
{\mathcal{T}}
–cocomplete
{\mathcal{T}}
–category equipped with a
{\mathcal{T}}
-distributive symmetric monoidal structure. Then
w_{\otimes}S^{1}\simeq S^{w_{+}}\in\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
.

Proof.

To prove (1), we first argue that we may write the category
(w_{+})_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\backslash\mathbbm{1}\simeq(\underline{\Delta}^{1}\times w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1})\backslash\mathbbm{1}
as a pushout. By the fact that colimits of a diagram of posets and fully faithful functors is just given by the strict colimit in 1–categories (cf. for example the argument in
[
16
, Obs. 3.2.4]
), the required pushout is given by
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{0\}\times(w_{*}\Delta^{1}\backslash\mathbbm{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{0\}\times w_{*}\Delta^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{1}\times(w_{*}\Delta^{1}\backslash\mathbbm{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(w_{+})_{*}\Delta^{1}\backslash\mathbbm{1}.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}

Hence, for a fixed
X\in V^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
, writing
\sigma_{X}\colon(w_{+})_{*}\Delta^{1}\backslash\mathbbm{1}\rightarrow V^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
for the diagram whose colimit is
\Sigma^{w_{+}}X
(i.e. the diagram given by
X
at the initial object and
0
everywhere else), we obtain by
Corollary

A.7
a pushout diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{\{0\}\times(w_{*}\Delta^{1}\backslash\mathbbm{1})}\sigma_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{\{0\}\times w_{*}\Delta^{1}}\sigma_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{\Delta^{1}\times(w_{*}\Delta^{1}\backslash\mathbbm{1})}\sigma_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{w_{+}}X.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}

But then it is straightforward to see that we have identifications

\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{\{0\}\times(w_{*}\Delta^{1}\backslash\mathbbm{1})}\sigma_{X}\simeq\Sigma^{w}X\quad\quad\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{\{0\}\times w_{*}\Delta^{1}}\sigma_{X}\simeq 0\quad\quad\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{\Delta^{1}\times(w_{*}\Delta^{1}\backslash\mathbbm{1})}\sigma_{X}\simeq\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{\{1\}\times(w_{*}\Delta^{1}\backslash\mathbbm{1})}\sigma_{X}\simeq 0.

Thus the colimit of the pushout diagram above is given by
\Sigma\Sigma^{w}X
, as claimed.

For (2), we will decompose the punctured cube
(u\sqcup w)_{*}\Delta^{1}\backslash\mathbbm{1}
and rewrite it as a parametrised colimit indexed by
(u_{+})_{*}\Delta^{1}\backslash\mathbbm{1}
. To this end, consider first the diagram
(u_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1})_{/-}\times w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\colon u_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\longrightarrow\underline{\mathrm{Cat}}
sending
a\mapsto(u_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1})_{/a}\times w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}
. The inclusion
w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\backslash\mathbbm{1}\hookrightarrow w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}
then induces the diagram

\overline{Q}\colon(u_{+})_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\simeq u_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\times\underline{\Delta}^{1}\longrightarrow\underline{\mathrm{Cat}}\quad::\quad(x,\epsilon)\mapsto\begin{cases}(u_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1})_{/a}\times(w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\backslash\mathbbm{1})&\text{ if }\epsilon=0\\
(u_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1})_{/a}\times w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}&\text{ if }\epsilon=1.\end{cases}

We claim that the colimit of the restricted diagram

Q\colon(u_{+})_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\backslash\mathbbm{1}\hookrightarrow(u_{+})_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\xlongrightarrow{\overline{Q}}\underline{\mathrm{Cat}}

is precisely the punctured cube
(u\sqcup w)_{*}\Delta^{1}\backslash\mathbbm{1}
. To see this, first note that all the maps encoded by the diagram
Q
are fully faithful, which is a consequence of the fact that for an object
a
in a poset
\underline{P}
, the map
\underline{P}_{/a}\rightarrow\underline{P}
is fully faithful because being sliced over an object is just a condition in posets. Secondly, by
Proposition

3.2.1
, the colimit of
Q
may be computed fibrewise as the ordinary colimit in
\mathrm{Cat}
. But again by
[
16
, Obs. 3.2.4]
, the colimit of a diagram of posets along fully faithful functors is just the poset given by the union of posets, which may thus be viewed as a full subcategory of
(u\sqcup w)_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}
. To conclude, just note that
(u\sqcup w)_{*}\Delta^{1}\backslash\mathbbm{1}
is fibrewise given by the subposet of
(u\sqcup w)_{*}\Delta^{1}\simeq u_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\times w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}
on those tuples
(a,b)
where either
a
is not the final object in
u_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}
or
b
is not the final object in
w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}
. This subposet is clearly precisely the one covered by the colimit of
Q
.

Given the claim, note first that since
(u_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1})_{/a}
has a final object, for any category
\underline{J}
, the colimit functor
\underline{\func}((u_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1})_{/a}\times\underline{J},V^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})\rightarrow V^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
is equivalently computed as the colimit functor
\underline{\func}(\{a\}\times\underline{J},V^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})\rightarrow V^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
. For a fixed
a\in u_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}
and
X\in V^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
, writing
\sigma_{X}\in\underline{\func}((u\sqcup w)_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\backslash\mathbbm{1},V^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})
for the diagram defining
\Sigma^{u\sqcup w}X
, the restrictions of
\sigma_{X}
to
(u_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1})_{/a}\times w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}
and
(u_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1})_{/a}\times(w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\backslash\mathbbm{1})
thus have the following colimits:

\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{(u_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1})_{/a}\times\underline{J}}\sigma_{X}\simeq\begin{cases}\Sigma^{w}X&\text{ if }a=\varnothing\text{ and }\underline{J}=w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\backslash\mathbbm{1}\\
0&\text{ else}.\end{cases}

Therefore, using the decomposition of the punctured cube above, we may factor the colimit functor
\underline{\func}((u\sqcup w)_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\backslash\mathbbm{1},V^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})\rightarrow V^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
by virtue of
Corollary

A.7
as

\underline{\func}((u\sqcup w)_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\backslash\mathbbm{1},V^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})\simeq\lim_{(u_{+})_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\backslash\mathbbm{1}}\underline{\func}(Q,V^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})\xlongrightarrow{\mathop{colim}\displaylimits}\lim_{(u_{+})_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\backslash\mathbbm{1}}V^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\xlongrightarrow{\mathop{colim}\displaylimits}V^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}

to compute
\Sigma^{u\sqcup w}X
as
\Sigma^{u_{+}}\Sigma^{w}X
, as required.

Finally, for (3), since we have a cofibre sequence
S^{0}\rightarrow 0\rightarrow S^{1}
, applying
w_{\otimes}
gives us a cofibre sequence

\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{w_{*}\Delta^{1}\backslash\mathbbm{1}}w_{\otimes}(S^{0}\rightarrow 0)\longrightarrow 0\longrightarrow w_{\otimes}S^{1}

by virtue of
[
16
, Prop. 3.2.8]
, whence the claimed equivalence as desired.
∎

3.3
Cubical excision

We show now that the parametrised cubes fit into the paradigm of
Section

2
. Let
{\mathcal{T}}
be atomic orbital.

Lemma 3.3.1
.

Let
w\colon W\rightarrow V
be a morphism in
\mathrm{Fin}_{{\mathcal{T}}}
. Then the cube
w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{{\mathcal{T}}_{/V}}
is a complementable parametrised poset.

Proof.

To see that
w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}
admits finite (co)products, just note that the diagonal functor
\Delta\colon\underline{\Delta}^{1}\rightarrow\underline{\Delta}^{1}\times\underline{\Delta}^{1}
admits both left and right adjoints since
\underline{\Delta}^{1}
admits finite (co)products. By for instance
[
15
, Lem. 4.3.2]
, we see that
w_{*}\Delta\colon w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\rightarrow w_{*}(\underline{\Delta}^{1}\times\underline{\Delta}^{1})\simeq w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\times w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}
also admits both left and right adjoints, whence the existence of finite (co)products in
w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}
. Now, ordinary cubes (i.e. finite products of
\Delta^{1}
in
\mathrm{Cat}
) are distributive and complementable. Since
w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}
are fibrewise cubes (i.e. fibrewise given by some finite product of
\Delta^{1}
’s) by
Fact

3.1.5
and (co)products are given by fibrewise (co)products by
[
29
, Prop. 5.8]
, we thus see that
w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}
is also distributive and complementable. This completes the proof.
∎

Proposition 3.3.2
.

Let
w\colon W\rightarrow V
be a morphism in
\mathrm{Fin}_{{\mathcal{T}}}
. Then the full subcategories
\sigma\colon(w_{!}\underline{\ast})^{\underline{\triangleleft}}\subseteq w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}
and
{(w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1})}\backslash\mathbbm{1}\subseteq w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}
are downward–closed. In particular, these two subcategories provide excisable poset structures to
w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}
.

Proof.

The case of
{(w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1})}\backslash\mathbbm{1}\subseteq w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}
is clear since this is fibrewise given by punctured cubes away from the final objects. To deal with the case
\sigma\colon(w_{!}\underline{\ast})^{\underline{\triangleleft}}\subseteq w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}
, by restricting if necessary (and using that singleton inclusions are stable under basechange by
Fact

3.1.9
(1), suppose we have a morphism in
w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}
with target in
(w_{!}\underline{\ast})^{\underline{\triangleleft}}
. We would like to show that the target lies in
(w_{!}\underline{\ast})^{\underline{\triangleleft}}
as well. This may be encoded as the dashed lifting problem in the left diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\Delta}^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(w_{!}\underline{\ast})^{\underline{\triangleleft}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\Delta}^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(w^{*}w_{!}\underline{\ast})^{\underline{\triangleleft}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\Delta}^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\Delta}^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\Delta}^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{tgt}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{tgt}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\sigma}}

By the
w^{*}\dashv w_{*}
adjunction, this is equivalent to the lifting problem in the right diagram. Now by construction of the map
\sigma
and using the notation of
Eq.

7
for the atomic orbital pullback, the map
\overline{\sigma}\colon(w^{*}w_{!}\underline{\ast})^{\underline{\triangleleft}}\simeq(\underline{\ast}\sqcup\overline{c}_{!}c^{*}\underline{\ast})^{\underline{\triangleleft}}\rightarrow\underline{\Delta}^{1}
is given by the maps
\underline{\ast}\xrightarrow{1}\underline{\Delta}^{1}
,
\overline{c}_{!}c^{*}\underline{\ast}\xrightarrow{0}\underline{\Delta}^{1}
, and
\varnothing\mapsto 0
. Thus, by a simple case analysis, it is easy to see via this description that the dashed lift in the right square above always exists, whence the dashed lift in the left square as desired.
∎

Example 3.3.3
.

By virtue of
Proposition

3.3.2
, we obtain the two types of excisable posets that will play a role in the sequel. Let
w\colon W\rightarrow V
be a morphism in
\mathrm{Fin}_{{\mathcal{T}}}
.

(a)

We call the tuple of data

\varphi_{w}\coloneqq\big(w_{*}\Delta^{1},\>\>\breve{(w_{*}\Delta^{1})}\coloneqq(w_{!}\ast)^{\underline{\triangleleft}},\>\>\varphi\colon(w_{!}\ast)^{\underline{\triangleleft}}\subseteq w_{*}\Delta^{1}\big)

the
singleton
w
–cube.

(b)

We call the tuple of data

\sigma_{w}\coloneqq\big(w_{*}\Delta^{1},\>\>\breve{(w_{*}\Delta^{1})}\coloneqq w_{*}\Delta^{1}\backslash\mathbbm{1},\>\>w_{*}\Delta^{1}\backslash\mathbbm{1}\subseteq w_{*}\Delta^{1}\big)

the
spherical
w
–cube,
the name being justified by
Construction

3.2.2
.

Definition 3.3.4
.

Let
w\colon W\rightarrow V
be a morphism in
\mathrm{Fin}_{{\mathcal{T}}}
,
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}},\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{{\mathcal{T}}}
categories having appropriate parametrised (co)limits, and
F\colon\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
a functor. We say that
F
is
singleton
w
–excisive (resp. spherically
w
–excisive)
if the morphism
V^{*}F\colon V^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\rightarrow V^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
in
\mathrm{Cat}_{{\mathcal{T}}_{/V}}
is
\varphi_{w}
–excisive (resp.
\sigma_{w}
–excisive), in the sense of
Definition

2.3.1
.

The following simple observation will be crucial for our purposes.

Observation 3.3.5
.

A spherically
w
–excisive functor is automatically also singleton
w
–excisive. This is an immediate inspection of
Definition

2.3.1
, as well as the fact that the inclusion
\varphi\colon(w_{!}\ast)^{\underline{\triangleleft}}\subseteq w_{*}\Delta^{1}
factors through the inclusion
w_{*}\Delta^{1}\backslash\mathbbm{1}\subseteq w_{*}\Delta^{1}
.

Remark 3.3.6
.

In the case of
{\mathcal{T}}=\mathcal{O}_{G}
, a precise comparison between our notions and those of the equivariant Goodwillie calculus developed in
[
8
]
is complicated by the fact that they are built on top of different models of parametrised
\mathcal{O}_{G}
-categories. However, taking a comparison between these foundations for granted, we expect that Dotto’s notion of excisiveness is different to the two notions of excisiveness defined above but is nevertheless expressible in our language.

Namely, in the case where
w\colon W\rightarrow G/G
is the unique
G
–equivariant map where
W=\coprod_{i}G/H_{i}
is a finite
G
–set, he defines in §2.1 of
op. cit.
the
star subcategory

\underline{\mathrm{St}}(W_{+})\subseteq(w_{+})_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}
as the
G
–subposet spanned by the proper subsets of
(G/H_{i})_{+}
for all
i
. Comparing
[
8
, Defs. 2.1 and 2.10]
, we expect a functor
F\colon\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
is
W
–excisive in Dotto’s sense if it is excisive in our sense using the excisable poset structure given by
\underline{\mathrm{St}}(W_{+})\subseteq(w_{+})_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}
. Note importantly that this really depends on the orbit decomposition of
W
as a
G
–set. Nonetheless, he shows in
[
8
, Prop. 2.34]
that this notion still enjoys certain compatibilities with restrictions to subgroups of
G
.

Dotto’s notion is related to the ones in
Definition

3.3.4
as follows: there are clearly inclusions
\left((w_{+})_{!}\ast\right)^{\underline{\triangleleft}}\subseteq\underline{\mathrm{St}}(W_{+})\subseteq(w_{+})_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\backslash\mathbbm{1}\subseteq(w_{+})_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}
. Thus, as in
Observation

3.3.5
, we see that spherical
w_{+}
–excisiveness implies Dotto–excisiveness implies singleton
w_{+}
–excisiveness. We shall see in
Remark

3.4.20
that all these notions collapses to the same condition when the target
\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
is equivariantly stable.

3.4
Semiadditivity and stability

In this final subsection, we put all the ingredients together and explain how to use singleton
w
–excisiveness to encode
w
–semiadditivity in the key
Lemma

3.4.13
. We then use
Observation

3.3.5
to prove the remaining two main theorems.

Before setting out on the work in earnest, let us remind the reader of the following standard observation from Goodwillie calculus as motivation and also as an ingredient we shall need later.

Observation 3.4.1
.

Let
{\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}}
be pointed categories with finite (co)products and
F\colon{\mathcal{C}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{D}}
be a reduced functor, i.e. it preserves the zero object. If it is 1–excisive, then it is a semiadditive functor, i.e. the canonically constructed map

F(X\sqcup Y)\longrightarrow F(X)\times F(Y)

is an equivalence. This is because the left square in
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\sqcup Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X\sqcup Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

is a pushout in
{\mathcal{C}}
(this is standard, but see also
Setting

3.4.2
for the more general fact in the parametrised setup). Hence, by 1–excisiveness of
F
, the right square is a pullback in
{\mathcal{D}}
, i.e.
F(X\sqcup Y)\simeq F(X)\times F(Y)
as claimed.

Setting 3.4.2
.

Suppose
{\mathcal{T}}
has a final object
V
and
w\colon W\rightarrow V
is the unique map. Suppose
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
is pointed, admits finite indexed (co)products, and suppose we have
\partial\colon\underline{\Delta}^{1}\rightarrow w^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
given by
(X\rightarrow\ast)
. Note that this is a singleton (co)cartesian diagram. Suppose we have a functor
F\colon\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
which is
w
–excisive and preserves zero objects.

From these, we can extract the two composites

\alpha\colon w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\xlongrightarrow{w_{*}\partial}w_{*}w^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\xlongrightarrow{w_{!}}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\quad\quad\beta\colon w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\xlongrightarrow{w_{*}\partial}w_{*}w^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\xlongrightarrow{w_{*}}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}.

(8)

By
Proposition

3.1.14
and its dual, these are a singleton cocartesian
w
–cube and a singleton cartesian
w
–cube, respectively. Note that
\alpha(\varnothing)\simeq w_{!}X
,
\alpha(\mathbbm{1})=w_{!}\ast\simeq\ast
,
\beta(\varnothing)\simeq w_{*}X
, and
\beta(W)\simeq w_{*}\ast\simeq\ast
. Here, the equivalences
w_{!}\ast\simeq\ast\simeq w_{*}\ast
is by the pointedness of
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
.

Observation 3.4.3
.

Let us unwind what the cube
\alpha\colon w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
looks like pointwise. Similar considerations also hold for the cube
\beta
. Consider the value of
\alpha
at the
f
–point
x\in w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}
. More precisely, consider

f_{!}\underline{\ast}\xlongrightarrow{x}w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\xlongrightarrow{w_{*}\partial}w_{*}w^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\xlongrightarrow{w_{!}}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}.

To work this out more explicitly, consider the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coprod_{i\in I}Z_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sqcup_{i}{g}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sqcup_{i}u_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

(9)

which yields the equivalence
f^{*}w_{*}\simeq\prod_{i}u_{i*}g_{i}^{*}
. Thus by adjointing over the
f
–point datum, we obtain

\underline{\ast}\xlongrightarrow{\overline{x}}f^{*}w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\simeq\prod_{i}u_{i*}g_{i}^{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\xlongrightarrow{\prod_{i}u_{i*}g_{i}^{*}\partial}\prod_{i}u_{i*}u_{i}^{*}f^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\xlongrightarrow{\sqcup_{i}u_{i!}}f^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}.

Since the map
\underline{\ast}\xlongrightarrow{\overline{x}}f^{*}w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\simeq\prod_{i}u_{i*}g_{i}^{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}
chooses either
0
or
1
in each of the
i
factors, we thus see that
\alpha
, when evaluated at the
f
–point
x
, gives

\coprod_{j\in J}u_{j!}g_{j}^{*}X

where
J\subseteq I
is a subset.

Example 3.4.4
.

Visually, in the nonequivariant case of a 3–cube, the map
\alpha
for the object
(A,B,C)\in{\mathcal{C}}^{3}
from
Eq.

8
encodes the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ast}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\vee C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\vee C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\vee B\vee C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\vee B}

which one can check by hand is singleton cocartesian. For the map
\beta
, one replaces all the
\vee
with
\times
in the diagram, and one can also check by hand that it is singleton cartesian.

The two cubical diagrams from
Eq.

8
are related by the semiadditivity norm map, as we construct next.

Construction 3.4.5

(Norm transformation on cubes)
.

We construct a transformation
\aleph\colon\alpha\Rightarrow\beta
which is a morphism in
\underline{\func}(w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})
which we will argue is pointwise given by the appropriate norm transformations. The typical construction of the norm map may be done internally to
{\mathcal{T}}
, giving a norm transformation
w_{!}\Rightarrow w_{*}\colon w_{*}w^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
, we may define
\aleph
simply as the transformation
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{*}w^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{*}\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Downarrow\text{ norm}}

Given this construction, we are left to argue that it is pointwise given by the usual norm maps. So let us consider an arbitrary
f
–point in the diagrams
\alpha
and
\beta
. We are thus considering the datum

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{!}\underline{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{*}w^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{*}\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Downarrow\text{ norm}}

(10)

which, by adjunction, is equivalently the datum of a morphism in
f^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
. To make this more explicit, consider the pullback
Eq.

9
. Hence, we get an equivalence
f^{*}w_{*}\simeq\prod_{i}u_{i*}g_{i}^{*}
. Thus, since the norm maps are stable under basechange, we obtain by adjointing
f_{!}
in
Eq.

10
the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\ast}\rightarrow f^{*}w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}w_{*}w^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}u_{i*}g_{i}^{*}w^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\simeq\prod_{i}u_{i*}u_{i}^{*}f^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}w_{*}\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sqcup_{i}u_{i!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times_{i}u_{i*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Downarrow\text{ norm}}

Therefore, we indeed see that evaluating the transformation
\aleph\colon\alpha\Rightarrow\beta
at the given
f
–point provides the norm map.

Furthermore, given a functor
F\colon\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
, we obtain a canonical transformation

F\alpha\xlongrightarrow{F\aleph}F\beta\xlongrightarrow{\text{ limit interchange }}\beta_{F}

(11)

of functors
w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
.

Proposition 3.4.6
.

Suppose
F\Rightarrow G\colon w_{*}\Delta^{1}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
is a natural transformation of two cartesian diagrams such that
F(\mathbbm{1})\simeq G(\mathbbm{1})
and
s^{*}F\simeq s^{*}G\colon s^{*}w_{*}\Delta^{1}\backslash\varnothing\rightarrow s^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
for all maps
s\colon U\rightarrow T
which are nonequivalences. Then
F
is naturally equivalent to
G
.

Proof.

Because
w_{*}\Delta^{1}\simeq(w_{*}\Delta^{1}\backslash\varnothing)^{\underline{\triangleleft}}
, we find that both
F
and
G
are right Kan-extended from this full subcategory. In particular it suffices to identify the two functors on this subcategory. This is precisely guaranteed by the hypotheses since the only global objects on
w_{*}\Delta^{1}
are
\varnothing
and
\mathbbm{1}
, and we have assumed that
F
and
G
agree on
\mathbbm{1}
as well as on the lower fibres.
∎

To prepare for our inductive proofs, we make some general observations about atomic orbital categories.

Definition 3.4.7
.

Let
{\mathcal{T}}
be an atomic orbital category. We say that
{\mathcal{T}}
is of length
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0}\cup\{\infty\}
if the longest chain of nonequivalent morphisms in
{\mathcal{T}}
is
n
, i.e. if we have morphisms
a_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}a_{2}\xrightarrow{f_{2}}\cdots\xrightarrow{f_{i}}a_{i+1}
which are all not equivalences, then
i\leq n
and there exists a length
n
chain of nonequivalences. We say that it has
finite length
if it has length
n
for some
n<\infty
.

Observation 3.4.8
.

Note that if
{\mathcal{T}}
has a final object and has length
0
, then
{\mathcal{T}}\simeq\ast
. Furthermore, note that if
f\colon U\rightarrow W
is a morphism in
{\mathcal{T}}
which is not an equivalence, then since the forgetful functor
f\colon{\mathcal{T}}_{/U}\rightarrow{\mathcal{T}}_{/W}
is conservative, writing
\ell_{U},\ell_{W}
for the lengths of
{\mathcal{T}}_{/U}
and
{\mathcal{T}}_{/W}
respectively, we have
\ell_{U}\leq\ell_{W}-1
. This allows us to perform inductive arguments based on the chain lengths if they are all finite.

Definition 3.4.9
.

Let
{\mathcal{T}}
be an atomic orbital category. We say that it is
locally short
if for every object
V\in{\mathcal{T}}
, the slice category
{\mathcal{T}}_{/V}
has finite length.

Notice that a locally short atomic orbital category which has a final object has finite length.

Remark 3.4.10
.

We claim that given two two objects
X,Y\in{\mathcal{T}}_{/V}
, if there exist maps
f\colon X\rightarrow Y
and
g\colon Y\rightarrow X
in
{\mathcal{T}}_{/V}
then
f
and
g
are equivalences.
Namely, we may consider the endomorphism
fg\colon X\rightarrow X
of
X
. Applying
[
6
, Lemma 3.37]
to
{\mathcal{T}}_{/V}
we find that
fg
is an equivalence. In particular,
(fg)^{-1}g
is a section of
f
. But then
f
(and so also
g
) is an equivalence, by
[
5
, Lemma 4.3.2]
.

In particular we conclude that the relation
X\leq Y
if and only if
\hom_{{\mathcal{T}}_{/V}}(X,Y)\neq\varnothing
defines a well-defined partial order on the isomorphism classes of
{\mathcal{T}}_{/V}
. We note that an atomic orbital category
{\mathcal{T}}
is locally short if and only if the partial order on
\pi_{0}({\mathcal{T}}_{/V})
has finite length.

Example 3.4.11
.

Examples of atomic orbital categories with finite lengths are the orbit category
\mathcal{O}_{G}
of a finite group
G
, the category
\mathrm{Epi}_{d}
of finite sets and surjections up to size
d
, and the category
\mathrm{Vect}^{\mathbf{k}}_{d}
of
\mathbf{k}
–vector spaces of dimension at most
d
and surjections, where
\mathbf{k}
is a finite field.

An example of a locally short atomic orbital category which is itself not of finite length is
\mathrm{Orb}
, the (2,1)-category of finite connected 1–groupoids and faithful maps (i.e. maps that induce injections on
\pi_{1}
). Another generic example is given by the proper orbit category of an infinite discrete group
G
, that is, the full subcategory of the orbit category on the finite subgroups.

Finally, the category
\mathrm{Epi}
of finite sets and surjections of arbitrary size is an an example of an atomic orbital category which is not locally short.

We may now return to the problem at hand. To state it, let us recall the following definition of Nardin
[
25
]
.

Definition 3.4.12
.

Let
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}},\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
be pointed
{\mathcal{T}}
–categories where
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
admits finite indexed coproducts and
\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
admits finite indexed products. A functor
F\colon\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
is said to be
{\mathcal{T}}
–semiadditive
if it preserves the zero objects, and for all maps
w\colon W\rightarrow V
in
\mathrm{Fin}_{{\mathcal{T}}}
, the norm transformations
Fw_{!}\Rightarrow w_{*}F
of functors
w_{*}W^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\rightarrow V^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
as in
Construction

3.4.5
are equivalences. If
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
is pointed and admits finite indexed (co)products, we say that it is
{\mathcal{T}}
–semiadditive
if the functor
\mathrm{id}_{\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}}\colon\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
is
{\mathcal{T}}
-semiadditive.

The following is the key lemma.

Lemma 3.4.13
.

Suppose
{\mathcal{T}}
is a locally short atomic orbital category. Let
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}},\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
be pointed
{\mathcal{T}}
–categories which are parametrised bicomplete. If a reduced functor
F\colon\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
is singleton
w
–excisive for all morphisms
w\colon W\rightarrow V
in
\mathrm{Fin}_{{\mathcal{T}}}
, then it is
\mathcal{T}
–semiadditive.

Proof.

Since
w
–excisiveness and
w
–semiaddivity are both local conditions, by replacing
{\mathcal{T}}
with
{\mathcal{T}}_{/V}
we may assume without loss of generality that
{\mathcal{T}}
has a final object and is of finite length, and
w
is a map to the final object. By
3.4.1
and
3.4.8
, the case of length
0
is true by assumption. We next induct on the length.

By
w
–excisiveness of
F
, the
w
–cube

F\alpha\colon w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\xlongrightarrow{w_{*}\partial}w_{*}w^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\xlongrightarrow{w_{!}}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\xlongrightarrow{F}\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}

is cartesian. By pointedness of
F
,
(F\alpha)(\mathbbm{1})\simeq\ast
. Hence, we claim that, by induction on lengths, the norm map
Eq.

11
witnesses an equivalence of
F\alpha
to

\beta_{F}\colon w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\xlongrightarrow{w_{*}\partial}w_{*}w^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\xlongrightarrow{w_{*}w^{*}F}w_{*}w^{*}\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}\xlongrightarrow{w_{*}}{\mathcal{D}}

when restricted to
w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\backslash\varnothing
. To prove the claim, by
Observation

3.1.4
, the only nonterminal points in
w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\backslash\varnothing
are
f
-points for some
f\colon Y\rightarrow V
not an equivalence. Using the notations from
Eq.

9
, by
Observation

3.4.3
, such a point looks like
\coprod_{j\in J}u_{j!}g_{j}^{*}X
for some subset
J\subseteq I
. By
Construction

3.4.5
, the transformation
F\alpha\Rightarrow\beta_{F}
is given by the norm map

F\left(\coprod_{j\in J}u_{j!}g_{j}^{*}X\right)\longrightarrow\prod_{j\in J}F\left(u_{j!}g_{j}^{*}X\right)\longrightarrow\prod_{j\in J}u_{j*}g_{j}^{*}FX

(12)

when evaluated on the
f
-point of
w_{*}\underline{\Delta}^{1}\backslash\varnothing
under consideration. By
3.1.8
and
3.4.1
, the singleton excisiveness hypothesis guarantees that the first map in
Eq.

12
is an equivalence. On the other hand, the norm map
F\left(u_{j!}g_{j}^{*}X\right)\rightarrow u_{j*}g_{j}^{*}FX
is also an equivalence for each
j\in J
by induction on lengths: by
Observation

3.4.8
, the length of
{\mathcal{T}}_{/Y}
is strictly shorter than that of
{\mathcal{T}}_{/V}
because
f\colon Y\rightarrow V
was not an equivalence, and so by induction hypothesis, the aforementioned norm map is an equivalence. This completes the proof of the claim.

But then now by
Setting

3.4.2
, the diagram
\beta_{F}
is cartesian. Since
F\alpha
is cartesian, we see by
Proposition

3.4.6
that

Fw_{!}X\rightarrow w_{*}w^{*}FX
is an equivalence also, as desired.
∎

Remark 3.4.14
.

For simplicity we have stated the previous result for
\underline{C},\underline{D}
parametrised bicomplete. However as the proof makes clear, if one takes more care it is possible to prove the statement under weaker assumptions on
\underline{C}
and
\underline{D}
.

Remark 3.4.15
.

We would like to apply
Theorem

2.3.12
to prove the next theorem using spherical excision with respect to the parametrised cubes
w_{*}\Delta^{1}
where
w\colon W\rightarrow V
is a map in
\mathrm{Fin}_{{\mathcal{T}}}
. Unwinding the formula in
2.3.5
and
2.3.7
, we see that if
F\colon\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
preserves final objects, then (implicitly basechanging
F
to a
{\mathcal{T}}_{/V}
–functor),

{T}_{\sigma_{w}}F\simeq\Omega^{w}F\Sigma^{w}\quad\quad{P}_{\sigma_{w}}F\simeq\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{n}\big(F\longrightarrow\Omega^{w}F\Sigma^{w}\longrightarrow\Omega^{2w}F\Sigma^{2w}\longrightarrow\cdots\big).

However, we do not know a priori that our spherically faithful category
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
will be spherically differentiable in the sense stipulated by
Theorem

2.3.12
since we do not know in general that limits over the punctured cubes
\mathring{(w_{*}\Delta^{1})}
commute with sequential colimits. Fortunately, this does not pose a problem since
\Omega^{w}\colon\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
commuting with sequential colimits suffices: from the proof of
Lemma

2.3.11
, the only place where differentiability is used is to perform the commutation
{P}_{\sigma}{T}_{\sigma}\simeq{T}_{\sigma}{P}_{\sigma}
. For this, it suffices that
\Omega^{w}
commutes with sequential colimits, since

\begin{split}{P}_{\sigma_{w}}{T}_{\sigma_{w}}F&\simeq\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{n}\big(\Omega^{w}F\Sigma^{w}\longrightarrow\Omega^{2w}F\Sigma^{2w}\longrightarrow\Omega^{3w}F\Sigma^{3w}\longrightarrow\cdots\big)\\
&\simeq\Omega^{w}\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{n}\big(F\Sigma^{w}\longrightarrow\Omega^{w}F\Sigma^{2w}\longrightarrow\Omega^{2w}F\Sigma^{3w}\longrightarrow\cdots\big)\\
&\simeq{T}_{\sigma_{w}}{P}_{\sigma_{w}}F\end{split}

as required. In particular, we may apply
Theorem

2.3.12
to the special case of the identity functor
\mathrm{id}\colon\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
if
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
were spherically faithful with sufficient parametrised (co)limits to get that the identity functor is spherically excisive.

We are now ready to gather the ingredients and prove
Theorem

B
.

Theorem 3.4.16
.

Suppose
{\mathcal{T}}
is a locally short atomic orbital category. Let
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
be a pointed
{\mathcal{T}}
–category which is parametrised bicomplete. If
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
is spherically faithful, then it is
\mathcal{T}
–semiadditive.

Proof.

Let
w
be a morphism in
\mathrm{Fin}_{{\mathcal{T}}}
. Since
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
was
w
–spherically faithful, we get that
\mathrm{id}\rightarrow\Omega^{w}\Sigma^{w}
is an equivalence. Thus, by the formula for
{P}_{\sigma_{w}}
from
Theorem

2.3.12
together with
Remark

3.4.15
, we see that the transformation
\mathrm{id}_{\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}}\rightarrow{P}_{\sigma_{w}}\mathrm{id}_{\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}}
is an equivalence, i.e. that
\mathrm{id}_{\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}}\colon\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
is spherically
w
–excisive. Hence, by
Observation

3.3.5
, we see that
\mathrm{id}_{\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}}
is also singleton
w
–excisive. Since
w
was arbitrary, we get that
\mathrm{id}_{\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}}
is singleton
w
–excisive for all
w
, and so by
Lemma

3.4.13
,
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
is parametrised semiadditive, as wanted.
∎

This then yields
Theorem

3.4.17
almost immediately. Before we state it, let us recall now Nardin’s
[
25
]
notion of parametrised stability for atomic orbital base categories
{\mathcal{T}}
: a
{\mathcal{T}}
–category
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
is said to be
{\mathcal{T}}
-stable if it is
{\mathcal{T}}
-semiadditive, admits parametrised fibrewise finite (co)limits, and
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}(V)
is a stable category for all
V\in{\mathcal{T}}
.

Theorem 3.4.17
.

Suppose
{\mathcal{T}}
is a locally short atomic orbital category such that
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
admits a
{\mathcal{T}}
-distributive symmetric monoidal structure. Let
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
be a pointed parametrised presentable
{\mathcal{T}}
–category.
Then
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
is parametrised stable if and only if it is spherically invertible.

Proof.

That spherical invertibility implies parametrised stability is the content of
Theorem

3.4.16
, since spherical invertibility implies in particular spherical faithfulness. For the converse, note by
Proposition

3.2.7
(1, 3) that

are idempotent algebras in parametrised presentable categories, classifying the properties of being parametrised stable and spherically invertible respectively. By

, the latter subcategory of presentable categories is contained in the former. Therefore the conclusion of

to0.0pt{\hskip-0.19133pt\raisebox{0.46193pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}$}\hss}\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}$}}}}_{\ast}[(S^{w})^{-1}]
clearly attains a
{\mathcal{T}}
–distributive symmetric monoidal structure. Therefore if the conclusion of the theorem holds,
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
also admits a
{\mathcal{T}}
–distributive structure. In particular the assumption in
Theorem

3.4.17
is necessary. In other words, parametrised stability agrees with spherically invertibility if and only if
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
admits a
{\mathcal{T}}
-distributive symmetric monoidal structure.

Unfortunately it is unknown to us if this is the case for all locally short atomic orbital categories
{\mathcal{T}}
. This was previously claimed by Nardin
[
26
, §3.5]
, but the proof is currently believed to have gaps and it is unclear if the result is true in full generality. The issues in the proof will be explained in the same forthcoming work of the first author, Branko Juran, and Natalie Stewart referred to before. Luckily, for
{\mathcal{T}}=\mathcal{O}_{G}
, the category
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
admits a
{\mathcal{T}}
-distributive symmetric monoidal structure by
[
4
, Theorem 5.5.1]
.

Finally, having shown the confluence of two natural notions of parametrised stability on categories under suitable hypotheses in
Theorem

3.4.17
, we end the main body of this work by complementing this with a similar confluence of natural notions for “parametrised linearity” on functors into stable categories which are spherically faithful. In particular, this extends the validity of
[
7
, Prop. 3.33]
to an arbitrary locally short atomic orbital base category
{\mathcal{T}}
.

For this, recall the notation of
w_{+}\colon W\sqcup V\rightarrow V
from
Proposition

3.2.7
associated to any map
w\colon W\rightarrow V
in
\mathrm{Fin}_{{\mathcal{T}}}
.

Theorem 3.4.19
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a locally short atomic orbital category,
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
a pointed parametrised bicomplete category,
\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
a parametrised presentable stable category which is spherically faithful, and
F\colon\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
a reduced functor preserving filtered colimits. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

F
is
\sigma_{w_{+}}
–excisive for every map
w\colon W\rightarrow V
in
{\mathcal{T}}
,

(2)

F
is
\varphi_{w}
–excisive for every map
w\colon W\rightarrow V
in
\mathrm{Fin}_{\mathcal{T}}
,

(3)

F
preserves parametrised colimits.

Proof.

Firstly, note that (1) is equivalent to requiring that
F
is
\sigma_{w_{+}}
–excisive more generally for
w\colon W\rightarrow V
in
\mathrm{Fin}_{{\mathcal{T}}}
by
Construction

2.2.4
and
Proposition

2.3.13
. Also, condition (2) implies
1
–excisiveness since this may equivalently be rephrased as
\varphi_{\ast_{+}}
–excisiveness. Next, by local shortness, we know that (2) implies (3) since
\varphi_{w}
–excisiveness implies
{\mathcal{T}}
–semiadditivity by
Lemma

3.4.13
and
1
–excisive functors preserving filtered colimits into a stable category implies preservation of ordinary colimits.

For the rest of the proof, since the notions of excisiveness is local in
{\mathcal{T}}
so that we will be working with the base category
{\mathcal{T}}_{/V}
anyway, we may assume that
w
maps
W
to the final object and write
F
instead of
V^{*}F
. To see that (1) implies (2), we argue that
F
is
\sigma_{w}
–excisive for all
w\colon W\rightarrow V
in
\mathrm{Fin}_{\mathcal{T}}
. This will in particular imply that
F
is also
\varphi_{w}
–excisive by
Observation

3.3.5
.

Since
F
is
\sigma_{w_{+}}
–excisive, we know that
F\rightarrow P_{\sigma_{w_{+}}}F\simeq\mathop{colim}\displaylimits\big(F\rightarrow\Omega^{w_{+}}F\Sigma^{w_{+}}\rightarrow\Omega^{2(w_{+})}F\Sigma^{2(w_{+})}\rightarrow\cdots\big)

is an equivalence. On the other hand, the sequential colimit is also the diagonal sequential colimit in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{w}F\Sigma^{w}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{2w}F\Sigma^{2w}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega F\Sigma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{w}\Omega F\Sigma\Sigma^{w}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{2w}\Omega F\Sigma\Sigma^{2w}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{2}F\Sigma^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{w}\Omega^{2}F\Sigma^{2}\Sigma^{w}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{2w}\Omega^{2}F\Sigma^{2}\Sigma^{2w}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}

where we have also used that
\Omega^{n(w_{+})}\simeq\Omega^{nw\sqcup n+}\simeq\Omega^{nw}\Omega^{n}
and
\Sigma^{n(w_{+})}\simeq\Sigma^{nw\sqcup n+}\simeq\Sigma^{nw}\Sigma^{n}
from
Proposition

3.2.7
. Hence, by siftedness, we may also compute
P_{\sigma_{w_{+}}}F
as

\begin{split}&\mathop{colim}\displaylimits\big(P_{\sigma_{w}}F\rightarrow P_{\sigma_{w}}\Omega F\Sigma\rightarrow P_{\sigma_{w}}\Omega^{2}F\Sigma^{2}\rightarrow\cdots\big)\\
&\simeq P_{\sigma_{w}}\mathop{colim}\displaylimits\big(F\rightarrow\Omega F\Sigma\rightarrow\Omega^{2}F\Sigma^{2}\rightarrow\cdots\big)\simeq P_{\sigma_{w}}P_{1}F.\end{split}

Here, the first equivalence uses that
\Omega^{w}\colon\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
preserves sequential colimits by spherical faithfulness. Hence, we get
F\simeq P_{\sigma_{w_{+}}}F\simeq P_{\sigma_{w}}P_{1}F
, and so
F
is in particular
\sigma_{w}
–excisive, as wanted.

Finally, to see (3) implies (1), just consider

\begin{split}P_{\sigma_{w_{+}}}F&\simeq\mathop{colim}\displaylimits\big(F\rightarrow\Omega^{w_{+}}F\Sigma^{w_{+}}\rightarrow\Omega^{2w_{+}}F\Sigma^{2w_{+}}\rightarrow\cdots\big)\\
&\simeq\mathop{colim}\displaylimits\big(F\rightarrow\Omega^{w_{+}}\Sigma^{w_{+}}F\rightarrow\Omega^{2(w_{+})}\Sigma^{2(w_{+})}F\rightarrow\cdots\big)\\
&\simeq\mathop{colim}\displaylimits\big(F\xrightarrow{=}F\xrightarrow{=}F\xrightarrow{=}\cdots\big)=F\\
\end{split}

where the first equivalence is by the formula from
Remark

3.4.15
and that
F
is reduced, the second is by hypothesis that
F
preserves parametrised colimits, and the third is since
\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
was spherically faithful, and so
\mathrm{id}\rightarrow\Omega^{w_{+}}\Sigma^{w_{+}}
are equivalences by
Theorem

3.4.17
. Hence,
F\simeq P_{\sigma_{w_{+}}}F
, and so it is
\sigma_{w_{+}}
–excisive.
∎

Remark 3.4.20
.

As explained in
Remark

3.4.18
, in the case of equivariant homotopy theory for finite groups, we may apply
Theorem

3.4.17
to get that a
G
–presentable category is
G
–stable if and only if it is spherically invertible. Thus, by
Theorem

3.4.19
and the last paragraph of
Remark

3.3.6
, we see in this case that a reduced, filtered–colimit–preserving functor
F\colon\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
with
\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
being
G
–presentable and
G
–stable satisfies furthermore Dotto’s excisiveness if and only if it is singleton/spherically excisive if and only if it preserves all
G
–colimits. And so all these notions collapse to a single one in this regime.

Appendix A
Decomposition of colimit diagrams

Suppose
\underline{I}
is a parametrised category which admits a decomposition into a colimit
\underline{I}\simeq\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{\underline{J}}\underline{I_{j}}
. In this appendix we explain how one can compute colimits over
\underline{I}
in two steps: first one computs the colimit over each
\underline{I}_{j}
, and then one computes the colimit of these objects over
\underline{J}
. We will deduce this via the approach suggested by Horev-Yanovski in
[
17
]
.

Definition A.1
.

Suppose
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}_{\bullet}\colon\underline{I}\to\underline{\mathrm{Cat}}_{\mathcal{T}}
is a functor of
{\mathcal{T}}
-categories. We define
p\colon\underline{\mathrm{UnStr}}(\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}_{\bullet})\to\underline{I}
to be the cocartesian unstraightening of
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}_{\bullet}
, in the sense of
[
29
, Rmk 7.2]
.

Definition A.2
.

Suppose
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}_{\bullet}\colon\underline{I}\to\underline{\mathrm{Cat}}_{\mathcal{T}}
is a functor of
{\mathcal{T}}
-categories. We define the
{\mathcal{T}}
-category
\laxlim(\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}_{\bullet})
to be the
{\mathcal{T}}
-category of sections of the unstraightening
p\colon\underline{\mathrm{UnStr}}(\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}_{\bullet})\to\underline{I}
. More formally
\laxlim(\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}_{\bullet})
is defined via the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\laxlim(\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}_{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\func}(\underline{I},\underline{\mathrm{UnStr}}(\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}_{\bullet}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\mathrm{id}_{\underline{I}}\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\func}(\underline{I},\underline{I}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{*}}

Remark A.3
.

Given an object
A\in{\mathcal{T}}
and an object
i\in\underline{I}(A)
, the composite

\underline{A}\xrightarrow{i}\underline{I}\xrightarrow{s}\underline{\mathrm{UnStr}}(\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}_{\bullet})

factors through
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}_{i}\coloneqq\underline{A}\times_{\underline{I}}\underline{\mathrm{UnStr}}(\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}_{\bullet})
, the fibre of
\underline{\mathrm{UnStr}}(\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}_{\bullet})
over
i\in\underline{I}
. We will identify
s(i)
with an object of
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}_{i}
in this way. An object of
\laxlim(\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}_{\bullet})
can be viewed as a family of such objects parametrised by
\underline{I}
.

Remark A.4
.

Observe that, just as in ordinary category theory,
\lim\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}_{\bullet}
is the full subcategory of
\laxlim(\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}_{\bullet})
spanned by the cocartesian sections. For more details see
[
15
, Thm. 3.4.2]
.

Note that any natural transformation
F_{\bullet}\colon\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}_{\bullet}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\bullet}
induces a functor
F\colon\laxlim(\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}_{\bullet})\rightarrow\laxlim(\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\bullet})
, via post-composition with the unstraightening of
F_{\bullet}
. Because this preserves cocartesian edges,
F
preserves cocartesian sections and hence restricts to a functor
F\colon\lim\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}_{\bullet}\rightarrow\lim\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\bullet}
.

Lemma A.5
.

Suppose
F_{\bullet}\colon\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}_{\bullet}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\bullet}
is a natural transformation such that for all
i\in\underline{I}
,
F_{i}\colon\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}_{i}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}_{i}
is a left adjoint with right adjoint
G_{i}\colon\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}_{i}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}_{i}
. Then
F\colon\laxlim(\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}_{\bullet})\to\laxlim(\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\bullet})
admits a right adjoint, which we denote by
G
. Given a section
s\colon\underline{I}\rightarrow\laxlim(\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\bullet})
and an object
i\in\underline{I}(A)
, the object
G(s)(i)
is equivalent to
G_{i}(s(i))
. Informally,
G
is given by applying the
G_{i}
pointwise to the section
s
.

Proof.

As observed in
[
29
, Rmk. 7.4]
, the
\infty
-category of cocartesian fibrations over
\underline{I}
can be identified with ordinary cocartesian fibrations over
\mathrm{Total}(\underline{I})
. One may check that under this correspondence,
\laxlim(\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}_{\bullet})
corresponds to sections of the cocartesian fibrations of the corresponding functor
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}_{\bullet}\colon\mathrm{Total}(\underline{I})\rightarrow\mathrm{Cat}
such that the restriction to the fibre of
\mathrm{Total}(\underline{I})
over any point
A\in{\mathcal{T}}
is cocartesian. Under these identifications the claimed result is a special case of
[
19
, Lem. 3.19]
.
∎

Now suppose that we are in the special case that
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}_{\bullet}
is the constant functor on a
{\mathcal{T}}
-category
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
. Then a simple calculation shows that
\underline{\mathrm{UnStr}}(\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}_{\bullet})
is given by
\pi_{2}\colon\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\times\underline{I}\rightarrow\underline{I}
, and so
\laxlim(\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}_{\bullet})
is equivalent to
\underline{\func}(\underline{I},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})
. Now suppose we are given a cone
F\colon\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\bullet}
of pointwise left adjoints. The previous result then gives an adjunction

F\colon\func(\underline{I},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})\leftrightarrows\laxlim(\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\bullet})\colon G.

We will now explain how to apply this to identify the right adjoint out of a limit.

Theorem A.6
.

Suppose
\underline{I}
is a
{\mathcal{T}}
-category,
\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\bullet}\colon\underline{I}\rightarrow\mathrm{Cat}_{\mathcal{T}}
is a diagram of
{\mathcal{T}}
-categories, and
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\bullet}
is a cone. Assume that for every
i\in\underline{I}
the functor
F_{i}\colon\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}_{i}
admits a right adjoint
G_{i}
, and that
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
admits all
\underline{I}
-limits. Then the functor
F\colon\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\rightarrow\lim\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\bullet}
admits a right adjoint, described as the composite

\lim\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\bullet}\xrightarrow{G}\underline{\func}(\underline{I},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})\xrightarrow{\lim_{\underline{I}}}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}.

Proof.

Consider the functor

\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\xrightarrow{\Delta}\underline{\func}(\underline{I},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})\xrightarrow{F}\laxlim(\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\bullet}).

By our assumptions this admits a right adjoint, given by the composite

\laxlim(\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\bullet})\xrightarrow{G}\underline{\func}(\underline{I},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})\xrightarrow{\lim_{\underline{I}}}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}.

We claim this restricts to the adjunction of the statement, for which it suffices to check that the first composite factors through
\lim(\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\bullet})
. The functor
\Delta
clearly sends objects to cocartesian sections, because any equivalence is cocartesian. We have previously observes that
F
does as well, see the discussion before Lemma
A.5
, and so the composite restricts as required.
∎

Note that the previous theorem admits an obvious dual statement, which we apply to immediately obtain the following corollary.

Corollary A.7

(Decomposition of colimits)
.

Consider a diagram
\underline{I}_{\bullet}\colon\underline{J}\rightarrow\mathrm{Cat}_{\mathcal{T}}
, and write
\underline{I}
for the colimit of
\underline{I}_{\bullet}
. Suppose
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
is some
{\mathcal{T}}
-category which admits
\underline{J}
-colimits and
\underline{I}_{j}
-colimits for all
j\in\underline{J}
. Then
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
admits
\underline{I}
-colimits and
\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{\underline{I}}\colon\func(\underline{I},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})\to\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
is equivalent to the composite

\underline{\func}(\underline{I},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})\simeq\lim_{\underline{J}}\underline{\func}(\underline{I}_{j},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})\xrightarrow{\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{\underline{I}_{j}}}\func(\underline{J},\underline{{\mathcal{C}}})\xrightarrow{\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{\underline{J}}}\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}.
